Universal tester for multiple independence testing and classical-quantum arbitrarily varying multiple access channel
Ayanava Dasgupta, Naqueeb Ahmad Warsi, Masahito Hayashi

Abstract
We study two kinds of different problems. One is the multiple independence testing, which can be considered as a kind of generalization of quantum Stein’s lemma. We test whether the quantum system is correlated to the classical system or is independent of it. Here, the null hypothesis is composed of states having the quantum system is correlated to the classical system in an arbitrarily varying form. The second problem is the problem of reliable communication over classical-quantum arbitrarily varying multiple access channels (CQ-AVMAC) and establishing its capacity region by giving multiple achievability techniques. We prove that each of these techniques is optimal by proving a converse. Further, for both these techniques, the decoder designed is a universal decoder and can achieve any rate pair in the capacity region without time sharing and also these decoders do not depend on the channel and therefore they are universal. Our result covers the case when the channel parameter is continuous, which has not been studied even in the classical case. Further, both these techniques can be easily generalized to the case when there are T ⁡ ( T > 2 ) T(T>2) senders. The design of each of these decoders is based on the study of multiple independence testing. This approach allows us to study the problem of reliable communication over CQ-AVMAC from the point of view of hypothesis testing. Further, we also give a necessary and sufficient condition for the deterministic code capacity region of CQ-AVMAC to be non-empty.



I
Introduction

I-A

Arbitrarily varying channel and its quantum extension

Most of the results in traditional information theory are based on the assumption that the source and the channel are independent and identically distributed. However, in the real-world scenario, these assumptions may not be true. To relax these assumptions
[
1
,
2
]
introduced the notion of arbitrarily varying source and channel and studied the two fundamental problems of information theory i.e. the problem of source compression for arbitrarily varying source (AVS) and the problem of reliable communication over an arbitrarily varying channel (AVC).
In arbitrarily varying channel settings,
the channel changes arbitrarily and is
chosen randomly from a finite collection of channels

\{W_{s}\}_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
, where
s\in\mathcal{S}
is the channel parameter.
In this setting, the sender and the receiver do not know
the value of the parameter
s
nor
the probability distribution for the parameter
s
.
When we have a transmission with length
n
,
the parameter is chosen differently as

s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
.
Therefore, we focus on the worst value of
the decoding error probability under the choice of
n
parameters

s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
.
In contrast, in the compound channel model
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
,
the sender and the receiver do not know the channel parameter

s
, but the parameter
s
is fixed during the communication with length
n
.
This kind of indeterminateness can appear even in the quantum channel setting
because it is extensively difficult to control quantum devices.
Therefore, the channel coding over AVC with the quantum setting
is a more challenging problem.

For the classical setting,
in
[
6
]
it was shown that the capacity of an AVC may be zero under a certain necessary and sufficient condition, termed as “Symmetrizability”.
However, when the sender and the decoder share randomness to decide
the choice of code, the capacity is always non-zero (positive), which is called the randomized code capacity.
The problem of reliable communication over AVC with the classical setting
has been studied in great detail, see for example,
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
.
When the channel has a classical input and a quantum output,
the channel is called a classical-quantum channel.
In this setting,
Ahlswede
[
16
]
studied the problem of reliable communication over classical-quantum arbitrarily varying channels (CQ-AVC) both for fixed codes and random codes and established its capacity.
The paper
[
17
]
made further progress.
However, these papers assume the finiteness of the set
\mathcal{S}
of
channel parameters.

I-B

Arbitrarily varying multiple-access channel and its quantum extension

Establishing quantum network communication is another key topic
in the area of quantum information.
As one of its key approaches,
we focus on multiple-access classical-quantum channel (CQ-MAC),
where we have multiple senders and one receiver.
Since CQ-MAC has a more complicated structure than
the conventional channel setting, it is very difficult to fix the channel parameters.
Therefore, classical-quantum arbitrarily varying multiple-access channel (CQ-AVMAC) is a more important problem setting.
This paper focuses on CQ-AVMAC, and
design two different kinds of
universal decoders
for CQ-AVMAC
whose method is explained later.
We use these decoders to first establish the randomized code capacity region of CQ-AVMAC, where
each sender is allowed to have shared randomness with the receiver.
We then, derandomize our code to establish the deterministic code capacity region.
We show that this derandomization is not possible under certain necessary and sufficient conditions which we term as “
\mathcal{X}
-symmetrizable”, “
\mathcal{Y}
-symmerizable” and “
\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}
-symmetrizable”.
These conditions are similar in spirit to their classical counterparts which were used in studying the problem of reliable communication over classical arbitrarily varying multiple-access channel (AVMAC)
[
18
,
19
,
20
]
.
We also prove a matching
converse
for this problem. To the best of our knowledge, the capacity region for CQ-AVMAC has not been established in the literature prior to this work.

I-C

Approach of asymmetric hypothesis testing

To accomplish the above achievements,
we focus on an approach of asymmetric hypothesis testing.
In
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
, it has been observed that the problem of reliable communication over a noisy channel is closely related to asymmetric hypothesis testing.
Recently, quantum Stein’s lemma
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
and its various generalizations
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
]
attracts much attention in the area of quantum information.
Our approach is more related to the independence test formulated in Hayashi and Tomamichel
[
31
]
, which imposes a constant constraint for the error probability of incorrectly accepting the alternative hypothesis
H_{1}
in a way similar to quantum Stein’s lemma.
To see this relation, we focus on compound channels in the CQ channels.

In the classical-quantum setting, Datta and Dorlas in
[
36
]
, came up with a construction of
code for a compound channel.
However, this code only works for a finite collection of possible classical-quantum channels. Later,
[
37
,
38
]
proposed a universal coding strategy for classical-quantum channels, which works
for compound channels composed of an infinite collection of possible classical-quantum channels.
In particular, in
[
37
]
, the construction of the decoder for this universal code uses Schur-Weyl duality
[
39
,
40
]
and the method of types and the encoder does not depend on the dimension of the output quantum system.
The independence test
[
31
]
also employs the same structure.
Later, the approach
[
37
]

has been extended to CQMAC in
[
41
]
.

To extend this approach to CQ-AVC and CQ-AVMAC,
we extend the result in
[
31
]
to
various settings of asymmetric hypothesis testing between arbitrarily varying sources. We design two kinds of tests to study this problem. In particular, the first test consists of a collection of
non-simultaneous
tests, where each test identifies a certain type of independence between the classical and quantum subsystems. However, the second test which we design is a
simultaneous
test (based on the ideas of
[
42
]
) and unlike the first test, it consists of only one test, which simultaneously identifies whether the quantum subsystem is correlated with each of the classical subsystems with high probability.

Then, employing the standard relation between
cq channel coding and asymmetric hypothesis testing,
we design
universal decoders

for CQ-AVC and for CQ-AVMAC.
Furthermore, our method for CQ-AVC covers the case when
the set of channel parameters
\mathcal{S}
is not a finite set.
This case was not covered by
[
16
,
17
]
.
In addition,
our analysis covers the case when the set
\mathcal{S}
forms a continuous set.
Table
I
summarizes
the relation between our target problems and existing studies.

of this paper

and

of

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
Section
II
formulates our key approach,
the independence test, and briefly summarizes our approach
for CQ-AVC and for CQ-AVMAC.
In Section
III
we mention the notations and facts used in this manuscript, in Section
IV
we give a proof for Theorem
1
.
In Section
V
, we use Theorem
1
to give an achievability proof for the problem of reliable communication over CQ-AVC.
In Section
VI
,
we generalize the independence test to the case with three subsystems, which is formulated as
the problem of “multiple independence testing”.
We study the “universal test” to solve this problem.
In Section
VII
we study CQ-AVMAC by giving an achievability and converse.
Thus, deriving its randomized code capacity region and in section
VIII
we derandomize the code obtained in section
V
and give a necessary and sufficient condition for it to be possible.

II
Independence test and brief summary of our approach

We build on the ideas developed in
[
37
]
to study the problem of independence testing defined below.
Throughout the manuscript we use
\mathcal{B}^{n}
to denote the quantum system that contains the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
.

Definition 1
.

(Independence Testing)
We consider a classical subsystem
X
of dimension

\left|\mathcal{X}\right|<\infty
, spanned by an orthonormal basis
\left\{|x\rangle^{X}\right\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
.
We choose a probability distribution
P
defined over
\mathcal{X}
, which introduces
the state
{\rho^{X}_{P}}:=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\overset{X}{|x\rangle\langle x|}

and the distribution
P^{n}(x^{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}P(x_{i})
.
Consider a collection of density operators
\left\{\rho_{x,s}^{\mathcal{B}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})
, where

\mathcal{S}
is a non-empty set and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
is a
finite-dimensional
Hilbert space. Now consider two collections

H_{0}:=\left\{\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}\right\}_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}
and
H_{1}:=\left\{{\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right\}_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})}
of classical-quantum states, such that
\forall s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\rho_{x^{n},s^{n}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}},

(1)

where the state
\rho_{x^{n},s^{n}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}
is defined to be
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{x_{i},s_{i}}^{\mathcal{B}}
for
x^{n}:=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
.
The aim here is to design a hypothesis test (which is a measurement in the quantum setting and for the sake of simplicity we assume it to be projective) that accepts
H_{0}
(Null Hypothesis) with a very large probability and accepts
H_{1}
(Alternative Hypothesis) with an arbitrarily small probability.

To handle a general case for the set
\mathcal{S}
of parameters,
we introduce the following assumption.

Assumption 1
.

Given such a real number
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists a finite subset
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{S}

to satisfy the following condition.
For any
s\in\mathcal{S}
, there exists an element
\bar{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}

such that any
x\in\mathcal{X}
satisfies

\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{x,s}\preceq(1+\varepsilon)\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{x,\bar{s}}.

(2)

Subsection
IV-B
discusses what case satisfies this assumption.
In this problem, similar to quantum Stein’s lemma,
we impose a constant constraint for the error probability of incorrectly accepting the alternative hypothesis
H_{1}
.
Using Schur-Weyl duality and the method of types, we obtain the following theorem:

Theorem
1
.

Assume that a collection of density operators
\left\{\rho_{x,s}^{\mathcal{B}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}

satisfies Assumption
1
.
Consider the two hypotheses
H_{0},H_{1}
mentioned in the statement of Definition
1
and a non-empty set
\mathcal{S}
. For
\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
we define

\beta_{n}(\varepsilon):=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\preceq\mathbb{T}_{n}\preceq\mathbb{I}\end{subarray}}\left\{\max_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]}\quad\bigg|\quad\max_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\mathrm{Tr}[(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n})\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}]\leq\varepsilon\right\}.

(3)

Then, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\beta_{n}(\varepsilon)=\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q},

(4)

where
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
is the set of distributions on
\mathcal{S}
, and
see (
12
) in Fact
4
for the definition of
I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}
.

In this paper, we use only the achievability of Theorem
1
, i.e.,

\geq
in (
4
),
to design a universal decoder for CQ-AVC and establish its randomized code capacity.
We show its achievability in Section
IV
, and show its converse part in Appendix
A
.

Our technique to CQ-AVC
differs from the approach used in
[
16
]
in two ways. Firstly, our technique is based on hypothesis testing and secondly, our decoder is a universal decoder which was not the case in
[
16
]
.
Here, the projective measurement, which is used as a hypothesis test, does not depend on
\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}
and needs to work
for any arbitrary bipartite correlated state in
H_{0}
over
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
.
Hence, our difficulty relies on
the universal choice of our test.
That’s why the authors term it as “independence test”.
We note here that in Definition
1
, both
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
are composite.

Here, we overview the relation with related problem settings.
Hayashi and Tomamichel studied a similar problem in
[
31
]
wherein,
only the hypothesis
H_{1}
is composite.
In particular, they considered an arbitrary bipartite correlated state
\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}
and studied an asymmetric hypothesis testing between the following two hypotheses:

\displaystyle H_{0}:={\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}}^{\otimes n},

\displaystyle H_{1}:=\left\{{\rho^{\mathcal{A}}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right\}_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})},

(5)

where
\rho^{\mathcal{A}}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{B}}\left[\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}\right]
.
That is,
H_{0}
is the
n
-copy state
{\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}}^{\otimes n}
of
\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}
.
The aim here is to design a measurement-based test that accepts
H_{0}
with very high probability and accepts
H_{1}
with arbitrarily small probability.
The paper
[
32
]
generalized these results under the classical setting by relaxing the condition for
H_{1}
,
which includes the testing of Markovian property.
The quantum generalized Stein’s lemma

[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
]

considers the more general case when

H_{0}
is composed of a single
n
-copy state and

H_{1}
satisfies several conditions like convexity etc.
In contrast, quantum Sanov theorem
[
33
,
34
]

discusses the case
when
H_{0}
is composed of various
n
-copy state and

H_{1}
is composed of a single
n
-copy state.
The paper
[
35
]
extended the above setting to
the case when
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
are composed of
multiple
n
-copy states.
However, in our problem,

H_{0}
and
H_{1}
are composed of more complicated states, which reflects
the arbitrarily varying channel parameter.
Both hypotheses
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
contain non
n
-copy states.
In particular, the main difference from
[
31
]
is the richer structure
of
H_{1}
as (
1
).

Also, in our case
\left|\mathcal{S}\right|
may not be finite.
To address CQ-AVMAC, we extend the above setting to the case when
the classical system
X
is composed of two subsystems.
We study testing for whether the first or second classical system
is independent of other systems, which is formulated as
the problem of “Multiple independence testing” and is discussed in detail in Section
VI
.
Table
II
summarizes
the relation between two target hypothesis testing problems
of ours and existing studies, which include
Theorem
1

and “Multiple independence testing”.
In fact, we give two different techniques to study this problem.
Both these techniques yield a universal POVM. In particular, in the first technique, we use Schur-Weyl duality
[
39
,
40
]
, the method of types and sandwiching of projectors to come up with a universal
non-simultaneous
test.
On the other hand in the second approach, we first use the test obtained in Theorem
1
and use techniques like “tilting” and “augmentation” (developed by Sen in
[
42
]
) to arrive at a universal
simultaneous
tester. We make a comparison between both these techniques in Table
III
.

The above two techniques allow us to construct a
universal simultaneous decoder
for CQ-AVMAC. Even though we study the problem of CQ-AVMAC for the case of two senders in this manuscript, we show that both the above techniques can easily be generalized to the case when there are
T
-senders (where
T>2
). We note here that the first technique does not require the Hilbert space to be enlarged to obtain a simultaneous decoder unlike the second technique (based on the techniques developed in
[
42
]
). However, the first technique requires sandwiching of
O(2^{T-1})
projectors to construct the decoding POVM for the case of
T(T>2)
senders. Further, the error analysis for this decoder is arguably simpler in comparison to the decoder obtained using the second technique.

III
Notations and Facts

We use
\mathcal{H}
to denote a finite-dimensional Hilbert space,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
to represent the set of all state density operators acting on
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
represents the set of all linear operators over
\mathcal{H}
(
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
). For any quantum system
A
over the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}

\left|\mathcal{A}\right|
represents the dimension of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
. Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{S}
be two finite sets denoting the source and state alphabets. For any integer
b>0
,
[b]:=[1,2,\cdots,b]
.
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is the set of probability distribution over
\mathcal{X}
.
\mathcal{T}_{n}(\mathcal{X})
is the set of empirical distributions with denominator
n
. For any quantum state
\rho
, we define
\mbox{supp}(\rho):=\mbox{Span}\{|i\rangle:\lambda_{i}>0\},
where
\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}
represent non-zero eigenvalues of
\rho
. Similarly, for any distribution
P
defined over a set
\mathcal{X}
,
\mbox{supp}(P):=\{x\in\mathcal{X}:P(x)>0\}.
For any sequence
x^{n}:(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, a permutation
\pi_{n}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathcal{X}^{n},
of length
n
is a map such that
\pi_{n}\left(x^{n}\right):=(x_{\pi_{n}^{-1}(1)},\cdots,x_{\pi_{n}^{-1}(n)}).
Let
S_{n}
be the permutation group containing all permutations of length
n
and it is also known as
n
-th symmetric group. For a sequence
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, we define the subgroup
S_{n,x^{n}}:=\left\{\pi_{n}\in S_{n}:\pi(x^{n})=x^{n}\right\}

Given two distribution
P\text{ and }Q
over the set
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mbox{supp}(P)\subseteq\mbox{supp}(Q)
, the relative entropy between
P
and
Q
is defined as,

D(P\|Q):=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\log\left(\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}\right).

Given two quantum states
\rho,\sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\mbox{supp}(\rho)\subseteq\mbox{supp}(\sigma)
, the quantum relative entropy (also known to be
quantum KL-divergence
) between
\rho
and
\sigma
is defined as,

D(\rho\|\sigma):=\mathrm{Tr}[\rho(\log(\rho)-\log(\sigma))].

A distance measure on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is defined by
\left\lVert{P-Q}\right\rVert=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|P(x)-Q(x)|
where
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
. For an operator
O\in\mathcal{L}(H)
,
{\left\lVert O\right\rVert}_{p}
is the operator Schatten-
p
norm
[
49
, Definition
2.6
]
of
O
.
The entropy of
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
defined as
H(P)=-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\log P(x)
. For a set
A
,
conv(A)
denotes the closed convex hull of the set
A
. For two normal operators
A
and
B
we will use the notation
\Pi:=\{A\succeq B\}
to denote a projection operator on the positive eigenspace of the operator
(A-B).

Definition 2
.

(Dirac delta function)
Given a non-empty set
\mathcal{S}
, for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
, we define the Dirac delta function or the delta distribution
\delta_{s}
on
s
as follows:

\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},\delta_{s}(s^{\prime}):=\begin{cases}1\quad\textnormal{if}\quad s^{\prime}=s,\\
0\quad\textnormal{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Definition 3
.

Given two functions
f,g:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f(n)
is said to be
o(g(n))
if,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{f(n)}{g(n)}=0

Definition 4
.

Given two functions
f,g:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f(n)
is said to be
\omega(g(n))
if,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{g(n)}{f(n)}=0

Definition 5

(Topological interior)
.

The topological interior
\mathbf{Int}\{A\}
of a subset
A
of a topological space
X
is defined as the largest open subset of
X
contained in
A
.

Definition 6
.

(Type and Set of all types)
A vector of integers
n^{d}:=\left(n_{1},n_{2},\cdots,n_{d}\right)
(where
d>0
) is called
a type of size
n
and length
d
if
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i}n_{i}=n.

T^{n}_{d}
is denoted as the set of all distinct types of size
n
and length
d
.

Definition 7
.

(Young diagram and Set of all Young diagrams)
A vector of integers
n^{d}:=\left(n_{1},n_{2},\cdots,n_{d}\right)
(where
d>0
) is called
a Young diagram of size
n
and depth
d

if
n_{1}\geq n_{2}\geq\cdots\geq n_{d}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i}n_{i}=n.

\Lambda^{n}_{d}
is denoted as the set of all distinct Young diagrams of size
n
and depth
d
.

Definition 8

(Frequency type classes)
.

For a non-empty finite set
\mathcal{X}
and an integer
n>0
, given a frequency (or, type) function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{N}
( such that
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{X}}f(i)=n
), we define a set
T_{\bar{f}}\subset\mathcal{X}^{n}
to be the frequency type class or type set corresponding to
\bar{f}
where
\forall i\in\mathcal{X},\bar{f}(i)=\frac{f(i)}{n}
as follows,

T_{\bar{f}}:=\left\{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}:\frac{\left\lvert\left\{k:x_{k}=i\right\}\right\rvert}{n}=\bar{f}(i),\forall i\in\mathcal{X}\right\}.

The above set
T_{\bar{f}}
is a permutation invariant set i.e. for every sequence
x^{n}:(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, under any permutation
\pi_{n}\in S_{n},

\pi_{n}\left(x^{n}\right)\in T_{\bar{f}}
where
\pi_{n}\left(x^{n}\right):=(x_{\pi_{n}^{-1}(1)},\cdots,x_{\pi_{n}^{-1}(n)}).

Definition 9

(Permutation operators)
.

[
50
, Definition 8]

Given a permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
and a Hilbert space
\mathcal{B}
, the permutation operator corresponding to
\pi
, denoted as
V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)
, is an unitary matrix acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
which satisfies the following :

V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)|\psi_{1}\rangle\otimes|\psi_{2}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|\psi_{n}\rangle=|\psi_{\pi^{-1}(1)}\rangle\otimes|\psi_{\pi^{-1}(2)}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|\psi_{\pi^{-1}(n)}\rangle,

(6)

for all
|\psi_{1}\rangle,|\psi_{2}\rangle,\cdots,|\psi_{n}\rangle\in\mathcal{B}
.
Equivalently, for any orthonormal eigenbasis
\left\{|j\rangle\right\}_{j\in[\left|\mathcal{B}\right|]}
of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{B}
,
V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)
can be given as following:

V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi):=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n}\in[\left|\mathcal{B}\right|]^{n}}|i_{\pi^{-1}(1)},i_{\pi^{-1}(2)},\cdots,i_{\pi^{-1}(n)}\rangle\langle i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n}|,

Thus,
V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)
satisfies the following property:

V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{n}){V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi)=\mathcal{O}_{\pi^{-1}(1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\pi^{-1}(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\pi^{-1}(n)},

(7)

for all
\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{n}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B})
. From (
6
), it directly follows that

V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi){V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi)={V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi)V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)=\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}.

(8)

Definition 10

(Iterative Matrix Multiplication)
.

Given
n
matrices ( for the sake of simplicity we assume them to be square matrices)
M_{1},M_{2},\cdots,M_{n}
,
\bullet_{i\in[n]}M_{i}
can be defined as follows,

\bullet_{i\in[n]}M_{i}:=M_{1}\cdot M_{2}\cdot M_{3}\cdots M_{n}.

Fact 1

(Direct Sum of vector spaces)
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{n}
be
n
orthogonal subspaces of a vector space
V
. Then, the n-fold direct sum (or direct sum) of the vector spaces
V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{n}
is defined as

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}:=\left\{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})\hskip 5.0pt|v_{i}\in V_{i}(1\leq i\leq n)\right\},

and,

\dim{\left\{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}\right\}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dim\{V_{i}\}.

For example, consider two vector spaces
V_{1}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
V_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
over
\mathbb{R}
. Then,
V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
is a 4-dimensional vector space over
R
. If we consider a vector
|a\rangle:=\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}\\
a_{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}
in
V_{1}
and another vector
|b\rangle:=\begin{bmatrix}b_{1}\\
b_{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}
in
V_{2}
, then
|a\rangle
in the 4-dimensional vector space
V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
can be defined with padding
|\dim\{V_{2}\}|
zeros below vector
|a\rangle
, making
|a\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}\\
a_{2}\\
0\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}
and similarly
|b\rangle
in
V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
would be
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
b_{1}\\
b_{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}
. Thus,
|a\rangle+|b\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}\\
a_{2}\\
b_{1}\\
b_{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}
.

Fact 2
.

[
40
]

T^{n}_{d}
(See Definition
6
) and
\Lambda^{n}_{d}
(See Definition
7
) satisfies the following upper-bound :

\left|\Lambda^{n}_{d}\right|\leq\left|T^{n}_{d}\right|\leq(n+1)^{d-1}.

(9)

Fact 3

(Petz Quantum Reǹyi Divergence
[
51
]
)
.

Consider
\rho,\sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
and
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,+\infty)
. Then, Petz quantum Reǹyi divergence between
\rho
and
\sigma
is defined as follows,

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}(\rho\|\sigma):=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\mathrm{Tr}\left[\rho^{\alpha}\sigma^{1-\alpha}\right]&{\mbox{if }}(\alpha<1\cap\rho\not\perp\sigma)\cup(\rho\ll\sigma),\\
+\infty&\mbox{else}.\end{cases}

Fact 4

(
\alpha
-Quantum Mutual Information
[
52
,
53
]
)
.

Assuming the problem setup mentioned in Definition
1
and given a probability distribution
Q
over
\mathcal{S}
, we consider the following states:

\displaystyle\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q,x}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}Q(s)\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{x,s},\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{X},

\displaystyle\rho^{X\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}Q(s)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\overset{X}{|x\rangle\langle x|}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{x,s}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\overset{X}{|x\rangle\langle x|}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q,x},

(10)

\displaystyle\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Tr}_{X}\left[\rho^{X\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q}\right]=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q,x}.

Then, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
we define
I_{\alpha}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}
as follows,

\displaystyle I_{\alpha}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}

\displaystyle:=\min_{\mu^{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})}D_{\alpha}(\rho^{X\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q}||\rho^{X}_{P}\otimes\mu^{\mathcal{B}})\overset{a}{=}-\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\log\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)(\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q,x})^{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right],

(11)

where
\rho^{X}_{P}:=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\overset{\mathcal{X}}{|x\rangle\langle x|}
and
a
follows from follows from Sibson’s identity.
See
[
52
, Lemma
3
in the supplementary material]
or

[
53
, Lemma
2
]
. Further,
I_{\alpha}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}
satisfies the following:

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 1}I_{\alpha}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}=I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}

\displaystyle:=D(\rho^{X\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q}||\rho^{X}_{P}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q}).

(12)

Fact 5

(Schur Weyl Duality
[
39
,
40
]
)
.

Given a Young diagram
\lambda\in\Lambda^{n}_{d}
, we denote
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}
as the irreducible representation of
SU(d)
and
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
as the irreducible representation of
S_{n}
characterized with respect to
\lambda
. Then, for an Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of dimension
d
, Schur duality can be given as

\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{n}_{d}}\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\lambda},

(13)

where
\forall\lambda\in\Lambda^{n}_{d}:\left|U_{\lambda}\right|\leq(n+1)^{\frac{r(r-1)}{2}}
.

Fact 6

(Hölder’s Inequality for operators
[
49
]
)
.

For two operators
A,B\in\mathcal{L}(H)
we have the following:

\mathrm{Tr}[AB]\leq{\left\lVert AB\right\rVert}_{1}\leq\min\{{\left\lVert A\right\rVert}_{\infty}{\left\lVert B\right\rVert}_{1},{\left\lVert A\right\rVert}_{1}{\left\lVert B\right\rVert}_{\infty}\}.

Further, we state a more general statement of Hölder’s Inequality for operators. Given two positive semidefinite operator
A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and two real numbers
p,q\geq 1
such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, we have the following:

\left|\mathrm{Tr}[AB]\right|\leq\left(\mathrm{Tr}[A^{p}]\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\mathrm{Tr}[B^{q}]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

(14)

Fact 7
.

Given a positive semi-definite operator
A
over a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and a density matrix
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
, we have,

\mathrm{Tr}[A\rho^{t}]\leq\left(\mathrm{Tr}[A^{\frac{1}{1-t}}]\right)^{1-t},

(15)

for all
t\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Given a
t\in[0,1]
assume
r=\frac{1}{1-t}
and
s=\frac{1}{t}
then from (
14
), we have the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[A\rho^{t}]

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathrm{Tr}[A\rho^{t}]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathrm{Tr}[A^{r}]\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}\left(\mathrm{Tr}[\rho^{ts}]\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathrm{Tr}[A^{\frac{1}{1-t}}]\right)^{1-t}(\mathrm{Tr}[\rho])^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{Tr}[A^{\frac{1}{1-t}}]\right)^{1-t}.

Fact 8

(Gao’s union bound
[
54
,
55
]
)
.

Let
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},\cdots,\Pi_{n}
be projectors over
\mathcal{H}
and
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
. Then,

\mathrm{Tr}(\Pi_{n}\cdots\Pi_{2}\Pi_{1}\rho\Pi_{1}\Pi_{2}\cdots\Pi_{n})\geq 1-4\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Tr}[\Pi^{c}_{i}\rho],

where
\Pi^{c}_{i}=\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{i}
.

Fact 9

(Gentle Measurement lemma under Ensemble of States
[
56
]
)
.

Let
\{p(x),\rho_{x}\}
be an ensemble and let
\bar{\rho}:=\sum_{x}p(x)\rho_{x}
. If an operator
E
, where
0\preceq E\preceq I
, has high overlap with the expected state
\bar{\rho}
i.e.
\mathrm{Tr}[E\bar{\rho}]\geq 1-\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Then,

\mathbb{E}_{X}\left[{\left\lVert\sqrt{E}\rho_{X}\sqrt{E}-\rho_{X}\right\rVert}_{1}\right]\leq 2\varepsilon^{1/2}.

Fact 10
.

[
57
, Theorem 6]

If
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{L})
and
\beta=(\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{L})
are two
L
-length sequences where
\forall i\in[L],\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in[0,1]
such that for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)

\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\alpha_{i}\geq 1-\varepsilon,\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\beta_{i}\geq 1-\varepsilon,

then

\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}\geq 1-2\varepsilon.

Fact 11
.

For the state
\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})
, we define
I[\mathcal{A};\mathcal{B}]_{\rho}
as follows,

I[\mathcal{A};\mathcal{B}]_{\rho}=\min_{\tau^{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})}D(\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}||\rho^{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\tau^{\mathcal{B}}),

(16)

where
\rho^{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{B}}[\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}]
and
I[\mathcal{A};\mathcal{B}]_{\rho}
is minimized when
\tau^{\mathcal{B}}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}[\rho^{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}]
.

Fact 12

(Hayashi Nagaoka inequality
[
21
]
)
.

If
A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
are two operators such that
B\succeq 0
and
0\preceq A\preceq\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{H}}
, then we have,

\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{H}}-(A+B)^{-\frac{1}{2}}A(A+B)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\preceq 2(\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{H}}-A)+4B.

Fact 13
.

(
[
43
, Proof of Lemma 2.3]
)
For any type set
T_{P}
of sequences in
\mathcal{X}^{n}
, assuming (
9
) Fact
2
, we have the following inequality:

P^{n}(T_{P})\geq(n+1)^{-(\left|\mathcal{X}\right|-1)}\geq(2n)^{-\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}.

Fact 14
.

[
49
]

Consider a positive operator
0\preceq\Pi\preceq\mathbb{I}
in
\mathcal{H}
and two states density operators
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
. Then,

\mathrm{Tr}[\Pi(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2})]\leq\frac{1}{2}{\left\lVert\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\right\rVert}_{1}.

Fact 15

(Fano’s inequality)
.

[
58
]
[Theorem 2.10.1]
Let
\hat{W}
and
W
be two random variables taking values over the set
\mathcal{W}
(
0<\left|\mathcal{W}\right|<\infty
) such that
\Pr\{W\neq\hat{W}\}\leq\varepsilon.
Then,

H_{b}(\varepsilon)+\varepsilon\log\left|\mathcal{W}\right|\geq H(W|\hat{W}),

where
H_{b}(\varepsilon)=-\varepsilon\log\varepsilon-(1-\varepsilon)\log(1-\varepsilon)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

Fact 16
.

[
42
, Corllary
1
]

Let
|h\rangle\in

\mathcal{H}
be a unit vector. Let
W_{0},W_{1},\cdots,W_{l}
be subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
.
\forall j\in[l]\cup 0
, define
\epsilon_{j}:={\left\lVert\Pi_{W_{j}}|h\rangle\right\rVert}_{2}^{2}
, where
\Pi_{W_{j}}
is the orthogonal projector on
W_{j}
and let
\alpha\in(0,1)
. we define two subspaces
\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{W_{\alpha}}
on
\mathcal{H}\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in[l]}\mathcal{H}_{j}
, as follows,

\mathbf{W}:=W_{0}+{\bigplus_{j=1}^{l}\mathcal{E}_{j,\alpha}(W_{j})},

where
\forall j\in[l],\mathcal{E}_{j,\alpha}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{j}
(where
\forall j\in[l]
,
\mathcal{H}_{j}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}
and
\left|\mathcal{H}_{j}\right|=\left|\mathcal{H}\right|
) is an isometric map defined as follows,

\mathcal{E}_{j,\alpha}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{1+\alpha^{2}}}}\mathcal{I}+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{{1+\alpha^{2}}}}\mathcal{E}_{j},

where
\mathcal{E}_{j}
is an isometry from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{H}_{j}
and
\mathcal{I}
is an identity embedding from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{H}\oplus\{\bigoplus_{j\in[l]}\mathcal{H}_{j}\}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\max\{\epsilon_{0},(1-\alpha)(\max_{j:j\in[l]}\epsilon_{j})\}

\displaystyle\leq{\left\lVert\Pi_{{\mathbf{W_{\alpha}}}}|h\rangle\right\rVert}_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{3l}{\alpha^{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{l}\epsilon_{j}.

(17)

Fact 17

(Chernoff Bound
[
59
]
)
.

Given a random variable
X
,
p\in\mathbb{R}
and some
\alpha>0
, we have

\Pr\{X\geq p\}=\Pr\{e^{\alpha X}\geq e^{\alpha p}\}\leq e^{-\alpha p}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\alpha X}\right].

IV
Construction of independence test

In this section, we construct independence test. That is,
we prove Theorem
1
.
We first construct it for a finite discrete set
\mathcal{S}
,
and later we construct it for a continuous set
\mathcal{S}
.

IV-A

Case with finite set
\mathcal{S}

To prove Theorem
1
given a discrete finite
\mathcal{S}
, we show that for any
\widehat{R}>0
and
t\in(0,1)
, there exists a projective-measurement based test
\mathbb{T}_{n}
which satisfies the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}+(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{X}\right|t}2^{nt\left(\widehat{R}-\min_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}\right)},\quad\forall s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n},

(19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|2^{-n\widehat{R}},\hskip 140.0pt\forall\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}),

(20)

where
\mathbb{T}_{n}
does not depend on the probability distribution
P
.
Since we can choose
t>0
such that

\widehat{R}-\min_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}<0
,
the pair of (
19
)
and (
20
) implies
Theorem
1
.

Here, we employ the same method that has been used in
[
37
]
. From Fact
5
we have,

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}}\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}.

Here, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda^{n}_{d}
, we denote
\Pi^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\lambda}
as the projection onto
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
and consider the following states:

\displaystyle\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}\right|}\Pi^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|}\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\lambda}.

(21)

Note that
\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}
is permutation invariant and it follows from the properties of Schur-Weyl duality (Fact
5
). Before proceeding to the proof of Theorem
1
, we require Lemma
1
mentioned below.

Lemma 1
.

[
37
, Equation (15)]

For any permutation invariant state
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
, we have

\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\preceq n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}.

We consider an
\bar{x}^{n}:=(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},\cdots,\bar{x}_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, which has a form
\bar{x}^{n}:=\left(\underbrace{1,\cdots,1}_{m_{1}},\underbrace{2,\cdots,2}_{m_{2}},\cdots,\underbrace{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|,\cdots,\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}_{m_{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}}\right)
, where
\forall i\in[\left|\mathcal{X}\right|],m_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i\in[\left|\mathcal{X}\right|]}m_{i}=n
. Given
\bar{x}^{n}
, consider the following state:

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\bar{x}^{n}}:=\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{m_{1}}}_{U,m_{1}}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{m_{2}}}_{U,m_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{m_{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}}}_{U,m_{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}}.

(22)

For any general
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, which is a permutation of
\bar{x}^{n}
i.e.
x^{n}=\pi(\bar{x}^{n})=(\bar{x}_{\pi^{-1}(1)},\bar{x}_{\pi^{-1}(2)},\cdots,\bar{x}_{\pi^{-1}(n)})
, for some
\pi\in S_{n}
, we define
\hat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}
as follows

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}:=V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)\left(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\bar{x}^{n}}\right){V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi),

(23)

where
V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)
is a permutation operator (see Definition
9
for formal definition of
V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)
). We observe the Lemma
2
mentioned below, which will be required in the proof of Theorem
1
.

Lemma 2
.

For any
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, we have
\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}=\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}
, where
\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}
and
\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}
are defined in
eqs.

21
and
23
.

Proof.

See Appendix
H
for the proof.

Given a probability distribution
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
, we define the following state:

\widehat{\rho}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P}:=\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}.

For
\widehat{R}>0
, we define a projective measurement-based test
\mathbb{T}_{n}
as follows,

\mathbb{T}_{n}:=\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\succeq 2^{n\widehat{R}}\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}\right\}\triangleq\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P}\succeq 2^{n\widehat{R}}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}\right)\right\},

(24)

where
{\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}:=\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}
. It is important to note that in (
24
), the first expression of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
is independent of the probability distribution
P
. Observe that
\widehat{\rho}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P}
and
{\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}
both are permutation invariant. Thus, the test
\mathbb{T}_{n}
is also permutation invariant which means
\forall s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n},\pi\in S_{n}

\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\rho_{P,\pi(s^{n})}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right].

(25)

Given an element
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
, we denote its corresponding empirical distribution (the empirical distribution which
s^{n}
follows) by
Q_{s^{n}}
and we denote the set
T_{Q_{s^{n}}}
as set of all sequences in
\mathcal{S}^{n}
which has the same type as
s^{n}
i.e.
T_{Q_{s^{n}}}=\left\{\pi(s^{n})\right\}_{\pi\in S_{n}}
. For a
\bar{s}^{n}\in T_{Q_{s^{n}}}
, from
[
58
, Equation (
11.15
)]
we have

Q^{n}_{s^{n}}(\bar{s}^{n})=2^{-nH(Q_{s^{n}})},

(26)

where
H(Q_{s^{n}})
is the Shannon entropy with respect to the distribution
Q_{s^{n}}
and we have the following,

\displaystyle Q^{n}_{s^{n}}(T_{Q_{s^{n}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t^{n}\in T_{Q_{s^{n}}}}Q_{s^{n}}(t^{n})\overset{a}{=}\left|T_{Q_{s^{n}}}\right|Q^{n}_{s^{n}}(\tilde{s}^{n}),

(27)

where
\tilde{s}^{n}
is any element in
T_{Q_{s^{n}}}
and
a
follows from (
26
). Thus, for any
\widehat{s}^{n}\in T_{Q_{s^{n}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|T_{Q_{s^{n}}}\right|}\overset{a}{=}\frac{1}{Q^{n}_{s^{n}}(T_{Q_{s^{n}}})}Q^{n}_{s^{n}}(\widehat{s}^{n})\overset{b}{\leq}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1}Q^{n}_{s^{n}}(\widehat{s}^{n}),

(28)

where
a
follows from (
27
) and
b
follows from Fact
13
. Thus, for any
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
, we upper-bound the quantity
\mathrm{Tr}[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}]
as follows,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}\right]\overset{a}{=}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\frac{1}{\left|T_{Q_{s^{n}}}\right|}\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}\rho_{P,\pi(s^{n})}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\frac{1}{\left|T_{Q_{s^{n}}}\right|}\sum_{\widehat{s}^{n}\in T_{Q_{s^{n}}}}\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,\widehat{s}^{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{b}{\leq}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\sum_{\widehat{s}^{n}\in T_{Q_{s^{n}}}}Q^{n}_{s^{n}}(\widehat{s}^{n})\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,\widehat{s}^{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\sum_{\widehat{s}^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}Q^{n}_{s^{n}}(\widehat{s}^{n})\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,\widehat{s}^{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{c}{=}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\left(\rho^{{X\mathcal{B}}}_{P,Q_{s^{n}}}\right)^{\otimes n}\right]

\displaystyle=(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x_{i}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1}\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}(x^{n})\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x_{i}}\right)\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\prec 2^{n\widehat{R}}\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{e}{\leq}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1}2^{nt\widehat{R}}\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}(x^{n})\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x_{i}}\right)(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}})^{-t}(\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n})^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{f}{\leq}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1}2^{nt\widehat{R}}n^{\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}(x^{n})\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x_{i}}\right)^{1-t}(\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n})^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}2^{nt\widehat{R}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}(x^{n})\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x_{i}}\right)^{1-t}\right)(\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n})^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}2^{nt\widehat{R}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\left(\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x}\right)^{1-t}\right)^{\otimes n}(\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n})^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{g}{\leq}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}2^{nt\widehat{R}}\left(\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\left(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\left(\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x}\right)^{1-t}\right)^{\otimes n}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-t}}\right]\right)^{1-t}

\displaystyle=(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}2^{nt\widehat{R}}\left(\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)\left(\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x}\right)^{1-t}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-t}}\right]\right)^{n(1-t)}

\displaystyle\overset{h}{=}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}2^{nt\left(\widehat{R}-I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q_{s^{n}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\overset{i}{\leq}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}+(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{X}\right|t}2^{nt\left(\widehat{R}-\min_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}\right)},

(29)

where
a
follows from the fact that
\mathbb{T}_{n}
is permutation invariant,
b
follows from (
28
),
c
follows from (
10
) of Fact
4
,
d
follows from the definition of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
in (
24
),
e
follows from the fact that since
\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}
commutes with
\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}
(which follows from Lemma
2
), we have
\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}-2^{n\widehat{R}}\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}\prec 0\right\}\leq 2^{nt\widehat{R}}\left(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\right)^{-t}\left(\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}\right)^{t}
for all
t\in(0,1)
,
f
follows from Lemma
3
mentioned below this paragraph,
g
follows from (
15
) of Fact
7
,
h
follows from (
11
) of Fact
4
and
i
follows from Fact
2
.
Therefore, we obtain (
19
).

Lemma 3
.

For all
t\in(0,1)
, for any
x^{n}:=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, we have the following operator inequality:

\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x_{i}}\right)^{t}(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}})^{-t}\preceq n^{\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}},

where the above notations follow from the above discussions.

Proof.

See Appendix
B
for the proof.

For any state
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
, we now upper-bound the quantity
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]
as follows,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{a}{=}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\left(\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}{V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi)\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{b}{\leq}n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{c}{\leq}n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|2^{-n\widehat{R}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\widehat{\rho}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P}\right]

\displaystyle\leq n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|2^{-n\widehat{R}},

(30)

where
a
follows from the fact that
\mathbb{T}_{n}
is permutation invariant. Thus, for any
\pi\in S_{n}
, the following holds,

\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]{=}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\left(V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}{V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi)\right)\right)\right].

In addition, step
b
follows from Lemma
1
and the fact that
\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}{V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi)
is permutation invariant and
c
follows from the definition of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
, given in (
24
).
Therefore, we obtain (
20
).

IV-B

Analysis on Assumption
1

We consider when Assumption
1
holds.
Clearly, when
\mathcal{S}
is a finite set, Assumption
1
holds.
Also, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 4
.

When the set
D_{x}:=\left\{\rho_{x,s}^{\mathcal{B}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}

is a compact subset of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})

for any
x\in\mathcal{X}

and any element
\rho_{x,s}^{\mathcal{B}}
is a full rank state.
Then, the set
\left\{\rho_{x,s}^{\mathcal{B}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}

satisfies Assumption
1
.

Proof.

We fix any real number
\varepsilon>0
.
For any element
x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S}
,
the subset
U_{x,s}:=\{\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{x,s^{\prime}}:s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{x,s^{\prime}}\preceq(1+\varepsilon)\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{x,s}\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})

is an open subset of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})

because the element
\rho_{x,s}^{\mathcal{B}}
is a full rank state.
Since
\cup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}U_{x,s}
equals
D_{x}
and is a compact set,
there is a finite subset
\mathcal{S}_{x,\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{S}
such that

\cup_{s\in\mathcal{S}_{x,\varepsilon}}U_{x,s}=D_{x}
.
Since the subset

\cup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{S}_{x,\varepsilon}
is a finite set,
the subset

\cup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{S}_{x,\varepsilon}
satisfies the
desired condition.

However, when the set
D_{x}
contains a pure state even when

\mathcal{S}
is a finite set,
the assumption of the above lemma does not hold.
Therefore,
the assumption of Lemma
4
is stronger than
Assumption
1
.

IV-C

Case with general set
\mathcal{S}

We assume Assumption
1
.
We choose an arbitrary real number

\widehat{R}
to satisfy

\displaystyle\widehat{R}<\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}.

(31)

We choose
t>0,\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle t\left(\widehat{R}-\inf_{Q}I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}\right)<-\log(1+\varepsilon).

(32)

From Assumption
1
,
for any
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
, there exists a
\bar{s}^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}_{\varepsilon}

such that any
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
satisfies

\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},s^{n}}\preceq(1+\varepsilon)^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},\bar{s}^{n}}.

(33)

Applying Theorem
1
on the set
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
, for any
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
, we upper-bound the quantity
\mathrm{Tr}[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}]
as follows

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}]

\displaystyle\overset{a}{\leq}(1+\varepsilon)^{n}\mathrm{Tr}[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,\bar{s}^{n}}]

\displaystyle\overset{b}{\leq}(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}+(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{X}\right|t}\left((1+\varepsilon)2^{t\left(\widehat{R}-I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q_{\bar{s}^{n}}}\right)}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}+(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{X}\right|t}\left(2^{\left(t\left(\widehat{R}-I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q_{\bar{s}^{n}}}\right)+\log(1+\varepsilon)\right)}\right)^{n},

(34)

where
a
follows from (
33
),

b
follows from (
19
).
From (
20
),
for any
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
, we directly have

\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]\leq n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|2^{-n\widehat{R}}.

(35)

Eq. (
32
) guarantees that (
34
) goes to zero. Since

\widehat{R}
is
an arbitrary real number to satisfy (
31
),
(
35
) implies

\lim_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\beta_{n}(\varepsilon)\geq\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}.

(36)

This gives a proof for Theorem
1
,
when
\mathcal{S}
is continuous.
As an application of Theorem
1
,
we study the problem of reliable communication over CQ-AVC in Section
V
.

V
Reliable Communication over Classical Quantum Arbitrarily Varying Channel (CQ-AVC)

V-A

Formulation

In this section, we explain how a code for
classical-quantum arbitrarily varying point-to-point channel (CQ-AVC)
can be constructed by using the independence test given in Theorem
1
.
For this aim, we formulate a CQ-AVC as follows.

Definition 11
.

(channel) We model a CQ-AVC between parties
Alice and Bob as a map

\mathcal{N}^{X^{n}\to B^{n}}_{s^{n}}:x^{n}\to\rho^{B^{n}}_{x^{n},s^{n}},

where
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
and
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
, (where
\left|\mathcal{X}\right|<\infty
and
\mathcal{S}
is a non-empty set). For each
x^{n}:=(x_{1}\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
and
s^{n}:=(s_{1}\cdots,s_{n})\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
,
we define the state
\rho^{B^{n}}_{x^{n},s^{n}}
to be
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{B}_{x_{i},s_{i}}
.
Further, both Alice (the sender) and Bob (the receiver) have no information about the sequence
s^{n}.

We aim to use this channel and enable Alice to transmit message
m\in[2^{nR}]
such that Bob can recover
m
with high probability for every
s^{n}
.

Definition 12
.

(Deterministic code)
An
(n,2^{nR})
-code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
for communication over a CQ-AVC consists of

•

an encoding function
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}:\mathcal{M}_{n}\to\mathcal{X}^{n}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{n}:=[2^{nR}]
,

•

a decoding POVM
\{\mathcal{D}^{(n)}_{m}:m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\}
.

An
(n,2^{nR})
-code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
for communication over a CQ-AVC is called
an
(n,2^{nR},\beta)
-code
when for any
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
,
the average probability
\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})
of error satisfies

\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n}):=\frac{1}{2^{nR}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}e(m,\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})<\beta,

where
e(m,\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n}):=1-\mathrm{Tr}[D^{(n)}_{m}\mathcal{N}^{X^{n}\to B^{n}}_{s^{n}}(\mathcal{E}^{(n)}(m))]
.

Definition 13
.

A number
R>0
is called an achievable deterministic code rate for a given CQ-AVC , if for any
\beta>0,\delta>0
and sufficiently large
n
, there exists a
(2^{nR},n,\beta)
deterministic code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
such that

\frac{1}{n}\log\left|\mathcal{M}_{n}\right|>R-\delta,

\sup_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})<\beta
.

The supremum over all such achievable rates is called the
deterministic code capacity

C_{d}
of a CQ-AVC. From
[
16
, Lemma 2]
we know that if
C_{d}>0
then

C_{d}=\max_{P}\inf_{Q}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q},

(37)

where
I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}
is defined in (
12
) of Fact
4
.

Definition 14
.

(Random code)
An
(n,2^{nR})
random code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
for communication over a CQ-AVC consists of

•

a random encoding function
\mathcal{E}^{(n),\gamma}:\mathcal{M}_{n}\to\mathcal{X}^{n}
, parameterized by a random variable
\gamma
, where
\mathcal{M}_{n}:=[2^{nR}]
,

•

a decoding POVM
\{\mathcal{D}^{(n),\gamma}_{m}:m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\}
, parameterized by a random variable
\gamma
.

In the above,
\gamma\sim G
is a random variable, shared between Alice and Bob and takes values over a finite set.

An
(n,2^{nR})
random code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
for communication over a CQ-AVC is called
an
(n,2^{nR},\beta)
random code
when for any
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
,
the average probability
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]
of error under the expectation of choices of random code (choices of
\gamma
) satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]:=\frac{1}{2^{nR}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[e(m,\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]<\beta,

where
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[e(m,\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]:=1-\mathbb{E}_{\gamma\sim G}\left[\mathrm{Tr}[D^{(n),\gamma}_{m}\mathcal{N}^{X^{n}\to B^{n}}_{s^{n}}(\mathcal{E}^{(n),\gamma}(m))]\right]
.

When the sender and the receiver share the randomness
\gamma
,
the random code
\mathcal{C}_{n}

can be realized.

Definition 15
.

A number
R>0
is called an achievable random code rate for a given CQ-AVC , if for any
\beta>0,\delta>0
and sufficiently large
n
, there exists a
(2^{nR},n,\beta)
random code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
such that

\frac{1}{n}\log\left|\mathcal{M}_{n}\right|>R-\delta,

\sup_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]<\beta
.

The supremum over all such achievable rates is called the
random code capacity

C_{r}
of a CQ-AVC.

Theorem
2
.

(
Random Code Capacity of CQ-AVC
)
Assume that a collection of density operators
\left\{\rho_{x,s}^{\mathcal{B}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}
satisfies Assumption
1
.
The random code capacity
C_{r}
of a CQ-AVC is equal to
\max_{P}\inf_{Q}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}
(where
I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}
is defined in (
12
) of Fact
4
).

When
\mathcal{S}
is a finite set,
the random code capacity
C_{r}
is known in
[
16
, Lemma 1]
.

For derandomization of our code, see Section
VIII
.

Proof.

The original proof of achievability for
[
16
, Lemma 1]
required an approximation lemma (see
[
16
, equation
10
]
), min-max theorem and typicality-based arguments. Our proof follows straightforwardly only from Theorem
1
. Further, unlike
[
16
]
our decoder is universal and it does not depend on the statistics of the channel. We now proceed with the proof.

(
Achievability
) We here show that for every rate
R<\min_{Q}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}
there exists a
(n,2^{nR},\varepsilon_{n})
random code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
for which the average error probability, averaged over the choice of code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
satisfies the following:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}:=\sup_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]\to 0.

(38)

V-A
1
Randomized Encoder Construction

Since

R<\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}
,
we choose
\widehat{R}
as

\displaystyle R<\widehat{R}<\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}.

(39)

Hence, we can choose

t>0
and
\epsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle t\Big(\widehat{R}-\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}\Big)<-\log(1+\epsilon).

(40)

Alice randomly generates
2^{nR}
independent input sequences of random
variables

\{X^{n}(m)\in\mathcal{X}^{n}:m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\}
according to
P^{n}
, where

P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.
That is, for any element
\forall m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,
the random variable
X^{n}(m)
obeys the distribution

\Pr\bigl\{X^{n}(m)\bigr\}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}P(X_{i}(m))
, where
X^{n}(m):=\left(X_{1}(m),\cdots,X_{n}(m)\right)
.
Then, we randomly choose the encoder
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}
subject to the following distribution;

\Pr(\mathcal{E}^{(n)}(m)=X^{n}(m)\hbox{ for }m=1,\ldots,2^{nR}):=\prod_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}\Pr\left\{X^{n}(m)\right\}.

If Alice has to send a message
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
, she encodes the message to the input sequence
X^{n}(m)
and sends a state
|X^{n}(m)\rangle^{X^{n}}
over the channel.

V-A
2
Decoding Strategy (Universal Decoder)

Upon receiving a quantum state
\rho_{\begin{subarray}{c}X^{n},s^{n}\end{subarray}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
, Bob tries to guess the message sent by the sender with the help of the POVM
\left\{\Lambda_{m}\right\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}
, where for each
m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,

\Lambda_{m}:=\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}T_{n,X^{n}(j)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}T_{n,X^{n}(m)}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}T_{n,X^{n}(j)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

where for any
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
,
T_{n,x^{n}}:=\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\succeq 2^{nR}\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}\right\}
(mentioned in (
24
)). Observe that the above POVM is universal as
T_{n,x^{n}}
is independent of the channel. If Bob gets the measurement outcome corresponding to
\Lambda_{\hat{m}}
, Bob then declares the message to be
\hat{m}
.
In the following, we denote the pair
(\mathcal{E}^{(n)},\left\{\Lambda_{m}\right\}_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{n}})
by
\mathcal{C}_{n}
.

V-A
3
Error Analysis

We now calculate the average error probability for the random codes
\mathcal{C}_{n}
for any particular
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]=\frac{1}{2^{nR}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[e(m,\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]=\frac{1}{2^{nR}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\Lambda_{m}\right)\left(\rho_{\begin{subarray}{c}X^{n}(m),s^{n}\end{subarray}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]\right]

\displaystyle\overset{a}{\leq}\frac{1}{2^{nR}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}\Biggl(2\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}-T_{n,X^{n}(m)}\right)\rho_{\begin{subarray}{c}X^{n}(m),s^{n}\end{subarray}}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\right]\right]+4\sum_{k\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus{m}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left[T_{n,X^{n}(k)}\rho_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}(m),s^{n}\end{subarray}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right]\right]\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{nR}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}\Biggl(2\sum_{X^{n}(m)}P^{n}(X^{n}(m))\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}-T_{n,X^{n}(m)}\right)\rho_{\begin{subarray}{c}X^{n}(m),s^{n}\end{subarray}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 25.0pt+4\sum_{k\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus{m}}\sum_{X^{n}(k)}P^{n}(X^{n}(k))\mathrm{Tr}\left[T_{n,X^{n}(k)}\sum_{X^{n}(m)}P^{n}(X^{n}(m))\left(\rho_{\begin{subarray}{c}X^{n}(m),s^{n}\end{subarray}}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\right)\right]\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{nR}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}\Biggl(4\sum_{k\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus{m}}\sum_{X^{n}(k)}P^{n}(X^{n}(k))\mathrm{Tr}\left[T_{n,X^{n}(k)}\left(\rho_{\begin{subarray}{c}s^{n}\end{subarray}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]+2\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n}\right)\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}\right]\Biggr)

\displaystyle\overset{b}{\leq}\frac{1}{2^{nR}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}\left(2(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}+(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{X}\right|t}2^{n\left(t\left(\widehat{R}-\min_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}\right)+\log(1+\varepsilon)\right)}+4\sum_{k\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\setminus{m}}\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbb{T}_{n}(\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{X}^{\otimes n}}_{P}\otimes\rho_{\begin{subarray}{c}s^{n}\end{subarray}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}})]\right)

\displaystyle\overset{c}{\leq}\frac{1}{2^{nR}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{nR}}\left(2(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}+(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{X}\right|t}2^{n\left(t\left(\widehat{R}-\min_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}\right)+\log(1+\varepsilon)\right)}+4\cdot 2^{nR}\cdot n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|2^{-n\widehat{R}}\right)

\displaystyle=2(n+1)^{\left|\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\right|-1+\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}+(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{X}\right|t}2^{n\left(t\left(\widehat{R}-\min_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I_{1-t}[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}\right)+\log(1+\varepsilon)\right)}+4\cdot 2^{n(R-\widehat{R})}\cdot n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|,

(41)

where
a
follows from Fact
12
,
b
follows from (
34
) and
c
follows from (
35
).
The combination of (
39
), (
40
), and (
41
) yields
(
38
).
This proves the achievability of Theorem
2
using the hypothesis testing approach.
This completes the proof.

(
Converse
)
We assume that a sequence of codes
\{\mathcal{C}_{n}\}
satisfy

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}:=\sup_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]=\sup_{Q_{S^{n}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}^{n})}\sum_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}Q_{S^{n}}(s^{n})\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}\left[\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},s^{n})\right]\to 0.

(42)

For an arbitrary element
Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
,
using Fano inequality (Fact
15
), we have

\displaystyle R-\frac{1}{n}H_{b}(\varepsilon_{n})-\varepsilon_{n}R\leq\frac{1}{n}I[M_{n};\mathcal{B}^{n}|\mathcal{C}_{n}]_{Q^{n}}\overset{a}{\leq}\frac{1}{n}I[X^{n};\mathcal{B}^{n}|\mathcal{C}_{n}]_{P_{X^{n}},Q^{n}},

(43)

where
a
follows from the Markov chain
M_{n}-X^{n}-\mathcal{B}^{n}
.
We denote the marginal distribution of
P_{X^{n}}

for
X_{j}
by
P_{X_{j}}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle R-\frac{1}{n}H_{b}(\varepsilon_{n})-\varepsilon_{n}R\leq\frac{1}{n}\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I[X^{n};\mathcal{B}^{n}|\mathcal{C}_{n}]_{P_{X^{n}},Q^{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}\sum_{j=1}^{n}I[X_{j};\mathcal{B}^{n}|X_{1},\ldots,X_{j-1}|\mathcal{C}_{n}]_{P_{X^{n}},Q^{n}}

\displaystyle\overset{b}{\leq}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}\sum_{j=1}^{n}I[X_{j};\mathcal{B}^{n},X_{1},\ldots,X_{j-1}|\mathcal{C}_{n}]_{P_{X^{n}},Q^{n}}

\displaystyle\overset{c}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}\sum_{j=1}^{n}I[X_{j};\mathcal{B}_{j}|\mathcal{C}_{n}]_{P_{X^{n}},Q^{n}}

(44)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}\sum_{j=1}^{n}I[X_{j};\mathcal{B}_{j}|\mathcal{C}_{n}]_{P_{X_{j}},Q}\leq\max_{P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q},

(45)

where
b
follows from
I[X_{j};X_{1},\ldots,X_{j-1}|\mathcal{C}_{n}]_{P_{X^{n}},Q^{n}}\geq 0
.
Since the random variable
S
subject to
Q^{n}
,
the channel
X^{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{n}
forms a discrete memoryless channel, which implies
the Markov chain
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{j-1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{j-1},\mathcal{B}_{j+1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n})-X_{j}-\mathcal{B}_{j}
.
This relation yields Step
c
.
Taking the limit, we obtain the desired inequality

\displaystyle R\leq\max_{P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}.

(46)

VI
Universal Test for Multiple Independence Testing

VI-A

Problem formulation

We generalize independence testing studied in Section
IV
to the case when
the classical system is composed of two classical systems.
Consider a collection of density operators
\left\{\rho_{x,y,s}^{\mathcal{B}}:x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y},s\in\mathcal{S}\right\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})
, where
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
are some non-empty finite sets and
\mathcal{B}
is a Hilbert space.
This collection of density operators satisfies Assumption
1
.
Now for each
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
we consider the following state:

\displaystyle\rho^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}},

(47)

where
\forall x^{n}:=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}:=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{n})\in\mathcal{Y}^{n},\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{x_{i},y_{i},s_{i}}^{\mathcal{B}}
,
P_{X}
and
P_{Y}
are probability distributions over
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, we here denote
X,Y
as the classical subsystems of respective dimensions
\left|\mathcal{X}\right|,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|
, corresponding to the random variables
X
and
Y
(i.e. through out this section,
X
and
Y
are used both as random variables and classical subsystems in appropriate contexts) spanned by orthonormal basis
\left\{|x\rangle^{X}\right\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
and
\left\{|y\rangle^{Y}\right\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}
respectively. Now consider the following hypotheses

\displaystyle H_{0}

\displaystyle:\left\{\rho^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}\right\}_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}},

(48)

\displaystyle H_{1,X}

\displaystyle:\left\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})\end{subarray}\end{subarray}},

(49)

\displaystyle H_{1,Y}

\displaystyle:\left\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})\end{subarray}\end{subarray}},

(50)

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle:\left\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})\end{subarray}\end{subarray}},

(51)

where
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}},\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}
and
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}
come from the respective marginals of the following state:

\displaystyle\sigma^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}},

(52)

and
\{\sigma_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}
is an arbitrary collection of states indexed by
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
and

y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}
(i.e.,
\{\sigma_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}}
).

The aim here is to design a measurement-based test
\mathbb{T}^{\star}_{n}
, which accepts
H_{0}
with very high probability and rejects
H_{1,X},H_{1,X}
and
H_{1}
with exponentially large probability. However, before solving this problem, we will study a weaker version of the above problem in the following subsection.
In addition, the converse part can be shown in the same way as Appendix

A
, we focus only on the direct part, i.e., the construction of
asymptotically good codes.

VI-B

Universal Non-simultaneous Test for Multiple Independence Testing

Here, our objective is to find two tests
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}^{X}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}^{Y}
which accept
H_{0}
(mentioned in (
48
)) with very high probability and accepts
H_{1}^{X}
and
H_{1}^{Y}
with arbitrarily small probability respectively, where
H_{1}^{X}
and
H_{1}^{Y}
is defined as follows,

H_{1}^{X}:=H_{1,X}\cup H_{1}\quad\text{ and }H_{1}^{Y}:=H_{1,Y}\cup H_{1}.

In the following, we construct the tests
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}^{X}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}^{Y}
which satisfies the objective mentioned above.

For an
\left(\bar{x}^{n},\bar{y}^{n}\right):=((\bar{x}_{1},\bar{y}_{1}),(\bar{x}_{2},\bar{y}_{2}),\cdots,(\bar{x}_{n},\bar{y}_{n}))\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n}
which has a form
\left(\bar{x}^{n},\bar{y}^{n}\right):=\Big(\underbrace{(1,1),\cdots,(1,1)}_{m_{1,1}},\underbrace{(1,2),\cdots,(1,2)}_{m_{1,2}},

\cdots,\underbrace{(\left|\mathcal{X}\right|,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|),\cdots,(\left|\mathcal{X}\right|,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|)}_{m_{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|}}\Big)
,
where
\forall i\in[\left|\mathcal{X}\right|],j\in[\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|],m_{i,j}\geq 0
and
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[\left|\mathcal{X}\right|]\\
j\in[\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|]\end{subarray}}m_{i,j}=n
.
Given
\left(\bar{x}^{n},\bar{y}^{n}\right)
, we consider the following state:

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\bar{x}^{n},\bar{y}^{n}}:=\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{m_{1,1}}}_{U,m_{1,1}}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{m_{1,2}}}_{U,m_{1,2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{m_{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|}}}_{U,m_{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|}},

(53)

where

\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{m_{i,j}}}_{U,m_{i,j}}
is defined in (
21
)
for any
i\in[\left|\mathcal{X}\right|],j\in[\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|]
.
Now for any general pair
(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n}
, which is a permutation of
(\bar{x}^{n},\bar{y}^{n})
i.e.
x^{n}=\pi(\bar{x}^{n})=(\bar{x}_{\pi^{-1}(1)},\bar{x}_{\pi^{-1}(2)},\cdots,\bar{x}_{\pi^{-1}(n)})
and
y^{n}=\pi(\bar{y}^{n})=(\bar{y}_{\pi^{-1}(1)},\bar{y}_{\pi^{-1}(2)},\cdots,\bar{y}_{\pi^{-1}(n)})
, for some
\pi\in S_{n}
, we define
\hat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}
as

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}:=V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)\left(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\bar{x}^{n},\bar{y}^{n}}\right){V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi).

(54)

We define
\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}
for any
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}
in an analogous way,
\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}
is defined for any
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
in (
23
). We now define the following states:

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P_{X},P_{Y}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}},

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P_{X}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}},

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}^{Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P_{Y}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}.

For
\widehat{R}_{1},\widehat{R}_{2}>0
, we define three projective measurement-based tests
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X},\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
as follows,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\succeq 2^{n\widehat{R}_{2}}\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\right\}\triangleq\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P_{X},P_{Y}}\succeq 2^{n\widehat{R}_{2}}\left({\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{Y}}}\otimes\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P_{X}}\right)\right\},

(55)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\succeq 2^{n\widehat{R}_{1}}\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}\right\}\triangleq\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P_{X},P_{Y}}\succeq 2^{n\widehat{R}_{1}}\left({\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{X}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{X}}}\otimes\rho^{Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P_{Y}}\right)\right\},

(56)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\succeq 2^{n(\widehat{R}_{1}+\widehat{R}_{2})}\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}\right\}\triangleq\left\{\widehat{\rho}^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,P_{X},P_{Y}}\succeq 2^{n(\widehat{R}_{1}+\widehat{R}_{2})}\left({\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{X}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{X}}}\otimes{\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{Y}}}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{U,n}\right)\right\}.

(57)

Observe that like
\mathbb{T}_{n}
defined in (
24
), the expressions of

\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X},\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}
, and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
are independent of
the forms of the distributions
P_{X}
and
P_{Y}
. We now define our tests
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}^{X}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}^{Y}
as follows,

\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}^{X}:=\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}\quad\text{ and }\quad\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}^{Y}:=\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X}.

(58)

Note that the constructions of
