\{W_{x,y,s}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y},s\in\mathcal{S}}
.

VIII-C
2
CQ-channel

Now, we start with the proof of necessity part of Lemma
8
.
Similar to
[
16
, Lemma 2]
,
we reduce the problem of Lemma
8

to the classical case.
We choose a POVM

\left\{\mathcal{O}_{z}^{\mathcal{B}}\right\}_{z=1}^{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{2}}

such that the operators
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\mathcal{B}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{2}}^{\mathcal{B}}

are linearly independent, i.e., they linearly span
the set of Hermitian matrices on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Then, we define the channel

P_{x,y,s}(z):=\mathsf{Tr\,}\mathcal{O}_{z}^{\mathcal{B}}\rho_{x,y,s}^{\mathcal{B}}
.
Since the linear map mapping a Hermitian matrix
X
to
the vector
(\mathsf{Tr\,}\mathcal{O}_{z}^{\mathcal{B}}X)_{z=1}^{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{2}}
is invertible,
the non-symmetrizable property of
the CQ-AVMAC
\left\{\rho_{x,y,s}^{\mathcal{B}}\right\}

is equivalent to
the non-symmetrizable property of C-AVMAC
\{P_{x,y,s}(z)\}
.
Therefore,

P_{x,y,s}(z)
is non-symmetrizable.
Also, since
the CQ-AVMAC
\left\{\rho_{x,y,s}^{\mathcal{B}}\right\}
satisfies
Assumption
1
,
the C-AVMAC
\{P_{x,y,s}(z)\}
also
satisfies Assumption
1
.
Applying Lemma
13
to
the C-AVMAC
\{P_{x,y,s}(z)\}
,
we can achieve the positive rate pair.
Since the capacity region of
the CQ-AVMAC
\left\{\rho_{x,y,s}^{\mathcal{B}}\right\}

contains
the capacity region of
the C-AVMAC
\{P_{x,y,s}(z)\}
,
this proves Lemma
8
.

VIII-D

Proof of Lemma
10

From Lemma
8
, it follows that
\mathbf{Int}\{\mathcal{R}_{d}\}\neq\varnothing
is equivalent to CQ-AVMAC being non-symmetrizable (none of the conditions mentioned in
eqs.

150
,
151
and
152
are satisfied). We will use the achievability result when the code is randomized (as discussed in Theorem
3
) and we will require to show that for any random code achieving a rate-pair
(R_{1},R_{2})\in\mathbf{Int}\{\mathcal{R}_{d}\}
,
there always exists a fixed code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
which achieves the rate-pair
(R_{1},R_{2})
as well i.e.,

\mathcal{R}_{d}\supseteq\mathbf{Int}\{\mathcal{R}_{d}\}
, which implies
the relation
\mathcal{R}_{d}\supseteq\mathcal{R}_{r}
.
We will accomplish this using the technique of elimination of randomness which is a standard tool used in computer science and has also been used in
[
11
,
12
,
18
,
14
,
19
,
20
,
16
]
.

VIII-D
1
Structure of our proof

In the following proof, we fix a rate-pair
(R_{1},R_{2})\in\mathbf{Int}\{\mathcal{R}_{d}\}
, and will show the existence of a fixed code
\mathcal{C}_{n}
which achieves the rate-pair
(R_{1},R_{2})
.
For this aim, we concatenate two codes by setting the real number
t
as
t>1/2
.
The first one has
n^{2t}
codewords and length
v(n):\omega(\log n)=o(n)
(where
v(n)\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty
,
o(\cdot)
and
\omega(\cdot)
are defined in Definition
3
and
4
respectively).
The second one has
2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}
codewords and
length
n
.
Then, concatenating them, we
construct a code
\hat{C}_{\text{concat}}
with
n^{2t}\cdot 2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}
codewords of length
(v(n)+n)
, which will be shown to achieve the rate-pair
(R_{1},R_{2})
.

VIII-D
2
First code construction

Since
\mathbf{Int}\left\{\mathcal{R}_{d}\right\}\neq\varnothing
,
for an arbitrary small real number
\mu>0
,
there exists a deterministic code with the form;

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:\left\{\left((\bar{x}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{1}),\bar{y}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{2})),\hat{D}_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\right):(\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2})\in[n^{t}]\times[n^{t}]\right\}

(187)

such that
the probability of decoding error satisfies

\max_{s^{v(n)}\in\mathcal{S}^{v(n)}}\bar{e}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},s^{v(n)}){\leq}\mu.

(188)

The existence of the above code can be shown as follows.
We choose an element
(R_{1}^{\prime},R_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathbf{Int}\left\{\mathcal{R}_{d}\right\}
.
Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log n}{v(n)}=0
,
the rates of the code
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
for the first and second messages
are smaller than
R_{1}^{\prime}
and
R_{2}^{\prime}
.
Hence, the above code exists.

VIII-D
3
Second code construction

From the achievability of Theorem
3
,
there exists a random code
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}

characterized by two random variables
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{1}

taking values in finite non-empty support sets

\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
that satisfies the following conditions.

(1)

The two random variables
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{1}
are subject to two probability distributions

G_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma_{1}),G_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\Gamma_{2})

over the support sets
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
, respectively.

(2)

The random code
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}
has the following form;

\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}=\left\{\left((x^{n}_{\gamma_{1}}(m_{1}),y^{n}_{\gamma_{2}}(m_{2})),D_{m_{1},m_{2}}^{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}\right):(m_{1},m_{2})\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\times[2^{nR_{2}}]\right\}.

(189)

(3)

The code
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}
satisfies
the condition

\max_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1}\sim G_{1},\\
\gamma_{2}\sim G_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}},s^{n})\right]<\frac{e^{\mu}-1}{e^{2}}.

(190)

When we choose the two random variables
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}

to be

\{X^{n}(m_{1})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}:m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\}

and
\{Y^{n}(m_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}:m_{2}\in[2^{nR_{2}}]\}
,
our proof of Theorem
3

guarantees the existence of the above code.
The choice of the two random variables
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
depends on
n
, but this dependency causes no problem in the latter discussion.

We now consider
n^{2t}
i.i.d random variable pairs
\left\{Z_{i,j}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n^{t}],j\in[n^{t}]\end{subarray}}
,
which take values in
\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}
such that
for any
(i,j)\in[n^{t}]\times[n^{t}],

\Pr\{Z_{i,j}=(\hat{\gamma}_{1},\hat{\gamma}_{2})\}=G_{1}(\hat{\gamma}_{1})\cdot G_{2}(\hat{\gamma}_{2}).

For any particular
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
,
since the random variables
\{e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{Z_{i,j}},s^{n})\}

are independent and identically distributed,
due to (
190
), any
(i,j)\in[n^{t}]\times[n^{t}]
satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z_{i,j}\end{subarray}}\left[e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{Z_{i,j}},s^{n})\right]<\frac{e^{\mu}-1}{e^{2}}.

(191)

Since
e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{Z_{i,j}},s^{n})\in(0,1)
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{2e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{Z_{i,j}},s^{n})}\right]

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k}}{k!}\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z_{i,j}\end{subarray}}\left[e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{Z_{i,j}},s^{n})^{k}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k}}{k!}\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z_{i,j}\end{subarray}}\left[e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{Z_{i,j}},s^{n})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{a}{\leq}1+\frac{e^{\mu}-1}{e^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k}}{k!}=1+\frac{e^{\mu}-1}{e^{2}}e^{2}=e^{\mu},

where
a
follows from (
191
).
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\text{err}(n,s^{n})

\displaystyle:=\Pr_{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\left\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n^{t}]\\
j\in[n^{t}]\end{subarray}}e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{Z_{i,j}},s^{n})>n^{2t}\mu\right\}

(192)

\displaystyle\overset{b}{\leq}e^{-2\mu n^{2t}}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n^{t}]\\
j\in[n^{t}]\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{2\cdot e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{Z_{i,j}},s^{n})}\right]\leq e^{-2\mu n^{2t}}e^{\mu n^{2t}}=e^{-\mu n^{2t}},

(193)

where
b
follows from Fact
17
where
\alpha>0
is a constant.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\left\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n^{t}]\\
j\in[n^{t}]\end{subarray}}e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{Z_{i,j}},s^{n})>n^{2t}\mu,~\exists s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}\right\}\leq\sum_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\text{err}(n,s^{n})\leq\sum_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}e^{-\mu n^{2t}}=|\mathcal{S}|^{n}e^{-\mu n^{2t}}\to 0.

(194)

Hence, there exists a subset
\left\{(\gamma_{1,i},\gamma_{2,j})\right\}_{(i,j)\in[n^{t}]\times[n^{t}]}\subset\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}
such that

\frac{1}{n^{2t}}\sum_{(i,j)\in[n^{t}]\times[n^{t}]}e(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{(\gamma_{1,i},\gamma_{2,j})},s^{n})\leq\mu,~\forall s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}.

(195)

VIII-D
4
Concatenated code

Concatenating the above two codes,
we now construct a new code
\hat{C}_{\text{concat}}
with

n^{2t}\cdot 2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}
codewords of length
(v(n)+n)
given as follows

\hat{C}_{\text{concat}}:=\left\{\left((\bar{x}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{1}),x^{n}_{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}}}(m_{1}),\bar{y}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{2}),y^{n}_{\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}(m_{2})),\hat{D}_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\otimes D_{m_{1},m_{2}}^{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}},\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}\right):\begin{subarray}{c}(\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2})\in[n]\times[n],\\
(m_{1},m_{2})\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\times[2^{nR_{2}}]\end{subarray}\right\}.

Here, the symbol
\bar{x}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{1}),x^{n}_{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}}}(m_{1})
expresses the
concatenation of

\bar{x}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{1})
and
x^{n}_{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}}}(m_{1})
.

For each
s^{v(n)+n}:=s_{1}^{v(n)}\cdot s_{2}^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{v(n)+n}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2t}\cdot 2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\\
\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\\
m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\\
m_{2}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{D}_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\otimes D_{m_{1},m_{2}}^{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}},\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}\right)\left(\rho_{\bar{x}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{1}),\bar{y}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{2}),s_{1}^{v(n)}}\otimes\rho_{x^{n}_{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}}}(m_{1}),y^{n}_{\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}(m_{2}),s_{2}^{n}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2t}\cdot 2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\\
\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\\
m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\\
m_{2}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{D}_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\cdot\rho_{\bar{x}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{1}),\bar{y}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{2}),s_{1}^{v(n)}}\right]\mathrm{Tr}\left[D_{m_{1},m_{2}}^{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}},\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}\cdot\rho_{x^{n}_{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}}}(m_{1}),y^{n}_{\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}(m_{2}),s_{2}^{n}}\right].

Now, for any
(\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2})\in[n^{t}]\times[n^{t}]
, if we consider,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{D}_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\cdot\rho_{\bar{x}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{1}),\bar{y}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{2}),s_{1}^{v(n)}}\right],

\displaystyle\beta_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\\
m_{2}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[D_{m_{1},m_{2}}^{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}},\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}\cdot\rho_{x^{n}_{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}}}(m_{1}),y^{n}_{\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}(m_{2}),s_{2}^{n}}\right],

then, from Fact
10
, the following holds,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2t}\cdot 2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\\
\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\\
m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\\
m_{2}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{D}_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\otimes D_{m_{1},m_{2}}^{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}},\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}\right)\left(\rho_{\bar{x}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{1}),\bar{y}^{v(n)}(\hat{m}_{2}),s_{1}^{v(n)}}\otimes\rho_{x^{n}_{\gamma_{1,\hat{m}_{1}}}(m_{1}),y^{n}_{\gamma_{2,\hat{m}_{2}}}(m_{2}),s_{2}^{n}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2t}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\\
\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\end{subarray}}\alpha_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\beta_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\overset{c}{\geq}1-2\mu,

where
c
follows from the following series of inequalities,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2t}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\\
\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\end{subarray}}\alpha_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\overset{d}{\geq}1-\mu,\quad\frac{1}{n^{2t}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\\
\hat{m}_{1}\in[n^{t}]\end{subarray}}\beta_{\hat{m}_{1},\hat{m}_{2}}\overset{e}{\geq}1-\mu,

where
d
and
e
follows from
eqs.

195
and
188
respectively. Thus, for the concatenated code
\hat{C}_{\text{concat}}
we have,

\max_{s^{v(n)+n}\in\mathcal{S}^{v(n)+n}}\bar{e}(\hat{C}_{\text{concat}},s^{v(n)+n})<2\mu.

(196)

Since
\mu
is an arbitrarily small number and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log(2^{nR_{1}}n)}{v(n)+n}=R_{1},\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log(2^{nR_{2}}n)}{v(n)+n}=R_{2},

(197)

the rate pair
(R_{1},R_{2})
is achievable.
This completes the proof of Lemma
10
.

Remark 4
.

In the single sender case,
we can show the lemma corresponding to Lemma
10

by modifying the proof of Lemma
10

so that
the size of Alice sends in the first code is
n^{2t}

and the size of Bob sends in the first code is
0
.

VIII-E

Proof of Lemma
11

VIII-E
1
Use of Proof of Lemma
10

We choose the set

\mathcal{S}_{n}:=\mathcal{S}\cap\left\{\left(\frac{k_{1}}{n},\ldots,\frac{k_{d}}{n}\right):k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
.
Since
\mathcal{S}
is a compact subset,

|\mathcal{S}_{n}|=O(n^{d})
.
Given an arbitrary small number
\mu>0
,
substituting
\mathcal{S}_{n}
into
\mathcal{S}
,
we apply the same discussion as the proof of Lemma
10
given in Subsection
VIII-D
.
In this case, only (
194
) requires a different discussion.
The upper bound given in (
194
) is calculated as

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}_{n}|^{n}e^{-\mu n^{2t}}=O(n^{nd})e^{-\mu n^{2t}}=O(e^{nd\log n-\mu n^{2t}})\to 0.

(198)

Therefore, due to (
196
), we have

\max_{s^{v(n)+n}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{v(n)+n}}\bar{e}(\hat{C}_{\text{concat}},s^{v(n)+n})<2\mu

(199)

for sufficiently large
n
.

VIII-E
2
Use of Assumption
2

We employ
Fidelity
F(\rho,\sigma):=\mathsf{Tr\,}|\sqrt{\rho}\sqrt{\sigma}|
,
Bure’s distance

d_{B}(\rho,\sigma):=\sqrt{2(1-F(\rho,\sigma))}
,
and sandwich Renyi divergence of order
1/2

D_{1/2}(\rho\|\sigma):=-\log F(\rho,\sigma)^{2}
.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\rho-\sigma\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{1-F(\rho,\sigma)^{2}}=\sqrt{1-e^{-D_{1/2}(\rho\|\sigma)}}.

(200)

Since
d_{B}(\rho_{x,y,s}^{\mathcal{B}},\rho_{x,y,\bar{s}}^{\mathcal{B}})^{2}
is approximated to be
Fisher information times
\|s-\bar{s}\|^{2}

[
61
, Eq.(6.33)]
,
Assumption
2
guarantees

\displaystyle\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\min_{\bar{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\max_{x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y}}d_{B}(\rho_{x,y,s}^{\mathcal{B}},\rho_{x,y,\bar{s}}^{\mathcal{B}})^{2}=O(\frac{1}{n^{2}}).

(201)

Remember that Assumption
2
contains
the compactness of
\mathcal{S}
, which implies the existence of the maximum.
Thus,

\displaystyle\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\min_{\bar{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\max_{x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y}}D_{1/2}(\rho_{x,y,s}^{\mathcal{B}}\|\rho_{x,y,\bar{s}}^{\mathcal{B}})=O(\frac{1}{n^{2}}).

(202)

Hence,

\displaystyle\max_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\min_{{\bar{s}}^{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{n}}\max_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}}D_{1/2}(\rho_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\|\rho_{x^{n},y^{n},\bar{s}^{n}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}})=O(\frac{1}{n}).

(203)

The combination of (
200
) and (
203
) implies

\displaystyle\max_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\min_{{\bar{s}}^{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{n}}\max_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}}\|\rho_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\rho_{x^{n},y^{n},\bar{s}^{n}}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\|_{1}\leq O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}).

(204)

The combination of (
199
) and (
204
) implies

\max_{s^{v(n)+n}\in\mathcal{S}^{v(n)+n}}\bar{e}(\hat{C}_{\text{concat}},s^{v(n)+n})<2\mu+O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}})

(205)

for sufficiently large
n
.
Since
\mu
is an arbitrarily small number,
the rate pair
(R_{1},R_{2})
is achievable.
This completes the proof of Lemma
11
.

IX
Conclusion

We have studied the problem of independence testing by building on the techniques developed in
[
37
,
31
]
.
The aim here is to test whether the quantum system is correlated with the classical subsystem or is independent of it.
We have solved this problem by obtaining a universal test that does not depend on the form of the classical-quantum state.
Further, our test has the property that it is permutation invariance.
This property plays an important role in studying this problem.
Using this test, we have studied the problem of reliable communication over a classical-quantum arbitrarily varying point-to-point channel (CQ-AVC) and
have obtained an optimal universal decoder for this problem.

Next, we have generalized the problem of “independence testing” where we have more than
2
hypothesis based on different types of independence between the quantum subsystem and classical subsystems.
We have studied this problem by designing two different types of hypothesis testing techniques.
In the first technique, we have designed a collection of non-simultaneous universal tests building on the techniques discussed in
[
37
,
31
,
41
]
, where each test classifies different types of independence between the quantum subsystem and classical subsystems.
In the second technique, we have constructed a simultaneous universal test building on the techniques discussed in
[
31
]
and
[
42
]
.
The simultaneous tests obtained in the second technique works like a union of the non-simultaneous universal tests obtained in the first technique.
However, both these techniques has their advantages and disadvantages as summarized later.

We have studied the problem of reliable communication over classical-quantum multiple-access-arbitrarily varying channels (CQ-AVMAC).
We have given a proof of achievability for the capacity region of CQ-AVMAC under randomization of codes by designing a simultaneous decoding POVM.
We have constructed two kinds of simultaneous decoders with the help of the universal tests (both simultaneous and non-simultaneous tests)
while studying the problem of generalized independence testing.
We have observed that both of these methods can be extended to the case of more than
2
senders.
We have established the optimality of the capacity region (under randomization) by proving the converse.
While the capacity region of CQ-AVMAC for fixed codes may be empty sometimes,
we have shown that it will always be non-empty under a certain necessary and sufficient condition, which we denote as “non-symmetrizability” condition.
We have then demonstrated a derandomization technique to derandomize any random code under the non-symmetrizability condition and finally establish a capacity region if a CQ-AVMAC satisfies this condition.

TABLE III:
Comparision between Technique
1
and Technique
2
with respect to the decoder for CQ-AVMAC

Comparison

Technique
1

Technique
2

Extension for
T
(
T>2
) senders.

It can be extended for
T
senders.

It can be extended for
T
senders using similar techniques required to prove
[
42
, Corollary 4]
.

Enlargement of the Hilbert spaces of subsystems

Enlargement of the Hilbert spaces of subsystems is not required.

Requires enlargement of the dimension of the Hilbert spaces of subsystems. This enlarged dimension grows exponentially with the number of senders.

Sandwiching of Projectors in the Decoding POVM

Sandwiching of
O(2^{T-1})
projectors required to construct the decoding POVM for the case of
T
senders.

The construction of decoder POVM does not require any sandwiching of projectors.

Tools involved

This technique is based on Schur-Weyl duality and the method of types.

This technique is based on Schur-Weyl duality, the method of types and geometric ideas developed in
[
42
]
,

Hypothesis Testing

The decoding POVM is obtained from multiple non-simultaneous tests.

The decoding POVM is obtained from a simultaneous test.

Universal

It is universal and therefore does not depend on the channel.

It is universal and therefore does not depend on the channel.

Error analysis

The error analysis is simple.

The error analysis is arguably mathematically tedious.

Time sharing

This is a simultaneous decoder and hence, does not require time sharing.

This is a simultaneous decoder and hence, does not require time sharing.

In table
III
, we have compared the two different techniques used to prove the achievability of Theorem
3
. In the table below, we use the term “Technique
1
” to denote the technique of non-simultaneous hypothesis test mentioned in Theorem
3
and the term “Technique
2
” to denote the technique of simultaneous hypothesis test mentioned in Appendix
F
.

Finally, we remark on the relation with the compound channel.
In the single-sender case,
the randomized code setting in a compound channel
can be done in the same way as Section
V

by restricting distributions
Q
to delta distributions.
However, in the deterministic code case,
we need derandomization.
Since symmetrizable case does not yield zero transmission rate
in a compound channel,
The discussion in Section
VIII
does not work for
in a compound channel.
Since the channel has a permutation invariant form
in a compound channel,
the papers
[
37
]
showed that
the random permutation of a constant composition code
simulates a randomized code.
In the multiple-sender case,
even under randomized codes,
the capacity region of compound channels
cannot be obtained by
the capacity region of arbitrarily varying channel
with restricting distributions
Q
to delta distributions
[
41
]
.
Hence, a simple modification of our approach in Section
VII

does not yield
the capacity region of a compound channel in this case.

Appendix A
Converse part of Theorem
1

We will show the converse part of Theorem
1
.
We show the inequality
\leq
in (
4
) by contradiction.
We assume that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\beta_{n}(\varepsilon)>\inf_{Q}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}.

(206)

Using

\displaystyle\max_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(\sigma^{\mathcal{B}})^{\otimes n}\right)\right]},

(207)

\displaystyle\sup_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}\mathrm{Tr}[(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n})\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,s^{n}}]

\displaystyle=\sup_{Q_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}^{n})}\mathrm{Tr}[(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n})\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q_{n}}]

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}\mathrm{Tr}[(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n})\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q^{n}}],

(208)

we have

\beta_{n}(\varepsilon)\geq\beta_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon):=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\preceq\mathbb{T}_{n}\preceq\mathbb{I}\end{subarray}}\left\{\max_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{T}_{n}\left({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(\sigma^{\mathcal{B}})^{\otimes n}\right)\right]}\quad\bigg|\quad\sup_{Q}\mathrm{Tr}[(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n})\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q^{n}}]\leq\varepsilon\right\}.

(209)

As

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}D_{\alpha}\Big(\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q^{n}}\Big\|\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(\sigma^{\mathcal{B}})^{\otimes n}\Big)\Big)=D_{\alpha}\Big(\rho^{X\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q}\Big\|\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}\Big)\Big),

(210)

Fact 4 guarantees that

\displaystyle\inf_{Q}I[X;\mathcal{B}]_{P,Q}=\lim_{\alpha\to 1+0}\inf_{(\sigma^{\mathcal{B}},Q)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}D_{\alpha}\Big(\rho^{X\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q}\Big\|\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}\Big)\Big).

(211)

Hence, we can choose
\alpha>1
such that

\displaystyle r:=\limsup_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\beta_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\beta_{n}(\varepsilon)>r_{\alpha}:=\inf_{(\sigma^{\mathcal{B}},Q)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})}D_{\alpha}\Big(\rho^{X\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q}\Big\|\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}\Big)\Big).

(212)

We choose
(\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{*},Q_{*})\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})

such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\beta_{n}(\varepsilon)>r_{\alpha}^{\prime}:=D_{\alpha}\Big(\rho^{X\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q_{*}}\Big\|\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{*}\Big)\Big).

(213)

Assume that a test
\mathbb{T}_{n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[(\mathbb{I}^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\mathbb{T}_{n})\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q_{*}^{n}}]\leq\varepsilon.

(214)

Using the information processing inequality of Petz Quantum
Rényi divergence in a way as Eq. (3.137) of
[
61
]
,
we have

\displaystyle(\alpha-1)nr_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(\alpha-1)nD_{\alpha}\Big(\rho^{X\mathcal{B}}_{P,Q_{*}}\Big\|\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\sigma_{*}^{\mathcal{B}}\Big)\Big)=(\alpha-1)D_{\alpha}\Big(\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q_{*}^{n}}\Big\|\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(\sigma_{*}^{\mathcal{B}})^{\otimes n}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\log\mathsf{Tr\,}\Big[\mathbb{T}_{n}\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q_{*}^{n}}\Big]-(\alpha-1)\log\mathsf{Tr\,}\Big[\mathbb{T}_{n}\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(\sigma_{*}^{\mathcal{B}})^{\otimes n}\Big)\Big].

(215)

Hence,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n}\log\mathsf{Tr\,}\Big[\mathbb{T}_{n}\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q_{*}^{n}}\Big]\geq\frac{-(\alpha-1)r_{\alpha}^{\prime}+\frac{(\alpha-1)}{n}\log\mathsf{Tr\,}\Big[\mathbb{T}_{n}\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(\sigma_{*}^{\mathcal{B}})^{\otimes n}\Big)\Big]}{\alpha}.

(216)

Since
the condition (
214
) and the definition of
r
in (
212
)
imply

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{-1}{n}\log\mathsf{Tr\,}\Big[\mathbb{T}_{n}\Big({\rho^{X}_{P}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(\sigma_{*}^{\mathcal{B}})^{\otimes n}\Big)\Big]\geq r
,
we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\mathsf{Tr\,}\Big[\mathbb{T}_{n}\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q_{*}^{n}}\Big]\geq\frac{(\alpha-1)(r-r_{\alpha}^{\prime})}{\alpha},

(217)

which implies

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathsf{Tr\,}\Big[\mathbb{T}_{n}\rho^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P,Q_{*}^{n}}\Big]=0.

(218)

Eq. (
218
) contradicts (
214
).

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
3
:

Given an
x^{n}:=(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, there always exists an an
\bar{x}^{n}:=(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},\cdots,\bar{x}_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, which has a form
\bar{x}^{n}:=\left(\underbrace{1,\cdots,1}_{m_{1}},\underbrace{2,\cdots,2}_{m_{2}},\cdots,\underbrace{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|,\cdots,\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}_{m_{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}}\right)
, where
\forall i\in[\left|\mathcal{X}\right|],m_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i\in[\left|\mathcal{X}\right|]}m_{i}=n
, such that
x^{n}:=\pi(\bar{x}^{n})
, for some
\pi\in S_{n}
. Now for
\bar{x}^{n}
, we consider the following state:

\displaystyle\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},\bar{x}_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},1}\right)^{\otimes m_{1}}\otimes\left(\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},2}\right)^{\otimes m_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\left(\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}\right)^{\otimes m_{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}}

\displaystyle\overset{a}{\leq}\prod_{i=1}^{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}m_{i}^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{m_{i}}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{m_{i}}}_{U,m_{i}}

\displaystyle\overset{b}{\leq}n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\bar{x}^{n}},

(219)

where
a
follows from Lemma
1
,
b
follows from the fact
\forall i\in[\left|\mathcal{X}\right|],m_{i}\leq n,\left|\Lambda^{m_{i}}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|\leq\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}
.
Now from (
7
) of Definition
9
, it directly follows that

\displaystyle\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},\bar{x}_{i}}\right){V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi)

\displaystyle\overset{a}{\leq}n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}(\pi)\left(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{\bar{x}^{n}}\right){V^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}}^{\dagger}(\pi)

\displaystyle\overset{b}{=}n^{\frac{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}},

where
a
follows from (
219
) and
b
follows from (
23
).
Now from the above inequality for any
t\in(0,1)
, we can write the following:

\displaystyle\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x_{i}}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle\leq n^{\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|}\left(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q_{s^{n}},x_{i}}\right)^{t}\left(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\right)^{-t}

\displaystyle\overset{a}{\leq}n^{\frac{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|(\left|\mathcal{B}\right|-1)\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}{2}}\left|\Lambda^{n}_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}\right|^{t\left|\mathcal{X}\right|},

where
a
follows from the fact that for any
t\in(0,1)
,
\left(\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\right)^{t}
is invertible since
\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}
is a positive operator. This completes the proof of Lemma
3
.
\blacksquare

Appendix C
Proof of Lemma
6

C-A

Organization of our proof

From Lemma
7
, we have three projectors (tests)
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X},\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
, all accepting
H_{0}
with very high probabilities and accepting
H_{1,Y},H_{1,X}
and
H_{1}
respectively with arbitrarily low probabilities. We design a projector (test)
{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}
, which satisfies the properties of
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X},\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
i.e.
\forall s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
, for all
\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
and
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
, for any
(\widehat{R}_{1},\widehat{R}_{2})
satisfying
eqs.

101
,
102
and
103
, for large enough
n
and arbitrary
\delta\in(0,1)
,
{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}
satisfies the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}\left(\rho^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}\right)\right]\geq 1-Poly(\delta),

(220)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}\left({\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{Y}}}\otimes\sigma^{X^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P_{X},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\}}\right)\right]\leq g_{1}(n,\left|\mathcal{X}\right|,\left|\mathcal{B}\right|)2^{-n\widehat{R}_{2}},

(221)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}\left({\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{X}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{X}}}\otimes\sigma^{Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}\}}\right)\right]\leq g_{2}(n,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|,\left|\mathcal{B}\right|)2^{-n\widehat{R}_{1}},

(222)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}\left({\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{X}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{X}}}\otimes{\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{Y}}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\right)\right]\leq g_{3}(n,\left|\mathcal{B}\right|)2^{-n(\widehat{R}_{1}+\widehat{R}_{2})}.

(223)

Our proof for
\mathbb{T}^{\star}_{n}
is motivated by the work of Sen
[
42
]
. Before giving this proof we first discuss some observations made in
[
42
]
.
One possible construction for
{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}
can be

{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}:=\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X}\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}.
However, for any
(x^{n},y^{n})\in(\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n})
, the quantum states
\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}},\widehat{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}
may not commute with each other and therefore,
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}
may not commute with each other. Hence, this construction will not work.

Another possible construction for
{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}
one may think of, is to first construct a projector over the span of the complements of the corresponding support spaces of the projectors
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X},\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}
,
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
and then, set
{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}
to be the complement of that projector. This idea is motivated by finding the intersection of two sets by finding the complement of the union of the complements of corresponding sets. Although this is a well-established concept in classical scenarios, in the quantum setting, the span (analog of “union” operation in classical setting) of the above-mentioned complements can be the whole Hilbert space and therefore
{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}
may not even exist. We discuss below, how to get around with this issue.

Suppose, we have three subspaces
\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2},\mathcal{W}_{3}
in
\mathcal{H}
, whose span is the whole Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
they are residing in. We keep
\mathcal{W}_{3}
as it is, but tilt
\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
very minutely in two mutually orthogonal directions which are also orthogonal to the parent Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
Here by ’tilt’-ing towards an orthogonal direction, we mean adding a small component of this particular direction with the original vector and then normalizing the resultant vector to keep the length of the resultant vector the same as the original vector. This phenomenon can be thought of as tilting the vector minutely toward that particular direction. In the upcoming discussion, we will formally explain this idea of tilting.
Let us now denote the tilted version of subspaces
\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
as
\mathcal{W}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\prime}
respectively.
This increases the dimension of the Hilbert space in which three subspaces
(
\mathcal{W}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{W}_{3}
) reside and now if we calculate the span it does not cover the enlarged Hilbert space.
It is important to note that since we have minutely tilted
\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
, for any vector
|h\rangle
, the individual projection lengths of
|h\rangle
on
\mathcal{W}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\prime}
do not vary much from the individual projection lengths on
\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
respectively.

In our proof, tilting maps play a key role.
A tilting map is nothing but a type of isometry which Sen termed in
[
42
]
,
it projects any statevector into a bigger Hilbert space while keeping its length unchanged and changing the direction of the vector minutely (defined in terms of
\delta
in the following discussion) towards some pre-defined direction. For example, tilting a vector in 2D (say having
X
and
Y
plane) towards
Z
plane (which is orthogonal to
X
and
Y
) in a very small angle.

C-B

Isometric tilting maps

We now proceed with the approach of Sen
[
42
]
, for the construction of
{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}
which behaves like an “intersection” of
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X},\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
and we will show that this
{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}
satisfies
eqs.

220
,
221
,
222
and
223

simultaneously
.

Using Sen’s approach in
[
42
, Section
4
]
while defining an intersection projector of a collection of projectors under a classical-quantum setting, we first consider a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes 2}
with dimension
\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}
which can be decomposed as follows:

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes 2}:=\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{X}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{Y}},

where
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{X}},\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{Y}}
are two mutually orthogonal finite-dimensional isomorphic Hilbert spaces with dimension
\left|\mathcal{A}\right|
(where
\left|\mathcal{A}\right|<\infty
), and
\left\{|a_{r}\rangle\right\}_{r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}
and
\left\{|b_{t}\rangle\right\}_{t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}
are orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{X}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{Y}}
respectively.

For each
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|],t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, we define two isometric maps
\mathcal{M}_{X,a_{r},\delta}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{X}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{Y,b_{t},\delta}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\oplus(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{Y}})
as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{X,a_{r},\delta}

\displaystyle:|h\rangle\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\delta^{2}}}(|h\rangle\oplus\delta|h\rangle|a_{r}\rangle),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{Y,b_{t},\delta}

\displaystyle:|h\rangle\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\delta^{2}}}(|h\rangle\oplus\delta|h\rangle|b_{t}\rangle)

for
|h\rangle
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
.
The two maps

\mathcal{M}_{X,a_{r},\delta}
and
\mathcal{M}_{Y,b_{t},\delta}

are two ”tilting” maps which
tilt any vector
|h\rangle
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
minutely
(parametrized by
\delta
) towards the directions of
a_{r}
and
b_{t}
respectively in significantly larger Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{X}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\oplus(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{Y}})
, respectively.

For each
(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}
and for
\delta\in(0,1)
, we define another tilting map
\mathcal{M}_{XY,a_{r},b_{t},\delta}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\oplus(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{X}})\oplus(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{Y}})
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{XY,a_{r},b_{t},\delta}

\displaystyle:|h\rangle\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+2\delta^{2}}}(|h\rangle\oplus\delta|h\rangle|a_{r}\rangle\oplus\delta|h\rangle|b_{t}\rangle)\hbox{ for }\forall|h\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}.

The above map tilts any vector
|h\rangle
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
minutely (parametrized by
\delta
) towards both directions of
a_{r}
and
b_{t}
in a significantly larger Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}:=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{X}}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{Y}}
.

The following lemma evaluates a small perturbation of quantum states under the tilting map
\mathcal{M}_{XY,a_{r},b_{t},\delta}
.

Lemma 14
.

For any state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
and any
(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}
,
we choose two states

\widehat{\rho}_{a_{r},b_{t},\delta}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}:=\mathcal{M}_{XY,a_{r},b_{t},\delta}\left(\rho\right)

and

\tilde{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\left[\rho\right]_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{n}\times\left|\mathcal{B}\right|}&\left[\mathbf{0}\right]_{\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{n}\times 2\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{n}}\\
\left[\mathbf{0}\right]_{2\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{n}\times\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{n}}&\left[\mathbf{0}\right]_{2\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{n}\times 2\left|\mathcal{A}\right|\left|\mathcal{B}\right|^{n}}\end{array}\right]_{\left|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\right|\times\left|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\right|},

(224)

where
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\left[\mathbf{0}\right]_{A\times B}
is a
0
-matrix of order
A\times B
with
0
in each entry. Note that we get
\tilde{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
by padding
0
-matrices with
\rho
.
Then, the following relation holds;

{\left\lVert\widehat{\rho}_{a_{r},b_{t},\delta}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}-\tilde{\rho}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\right\rVert}_{1}\leq 8\delta.

This lemma will be shown in Subsection
C-G
.

C-C

Tilting and Augmenting the classical-quantum states

For each
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
, for a particular
(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
and
(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}
,
we tilt the state
\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}}
using the tilting map
\mathcal{M}_{XY,a_{r},b_{t},\delta}
and we obtain the following state:

\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),s^{n},\delta}:=\mathcal{M}_{XY,a_{r},b_{t},\delta}(\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}}).

(225)

Note that for each
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
, for a particular
(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n}
, we get a collection of tilted states
\left\{\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),s^{n},\delta}\right\}_{(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}}
. For each
(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n}
, to incorporate the collection of states
\left\{\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),s^{n},\delta}\right\}_{(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}}
into a single CQ state, we need to augment (or, enlarge) the classical subsystems

\mathcal{H}_{X^{n}},\mathcal{H}_{Y^{n}}
in the following way:

\rho^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}:=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\\
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|],t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{\prime}}{|x^{n},a_{r}\rangle\langle x^{n},a_{r}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{\prime}}{|y^{n},b_{t}\rangle\langle y^{n},b_{t}|}\otimes\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),s^{n},\delta},

(226)

where
\mathcal{H}_{X^{\prime}}:=\mathcal{H}_{X^{n}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{X}},\mathcal{H}_{Y^{\prime}}:=\mathcal{H}_{Y^{n}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{Y}}
.
Similarly, for
\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
,
we define the following tilted and augmented CQ state:

\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}}:=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\\
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|],t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{\prime}}{|x^{n},a_{r}\rangle\langle x^{n},a_{r}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{\prime}}{|y^{n},b_{t}\rangle\langle y^{n},b_{t}|}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta},

(227)

where
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}:=\mathcal{M}_{XY,a_{r},b_{t},\delta}\left(\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\right)
.

Lemma
14
shown in Subsection
C-G
guarantees
that the above-mentioned method of tilting does not perturb the quantum state much.
That is, Lemma
14
guarantees the following relations

\displaystyle{\left\lVert\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),s^{n},\delta}-\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}}\right\rVert}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 8\delta,\forall s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n},

(228)

\displaystyle{\left\lVert\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}-\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\right\rVert}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 8\delta.

(229)

for each
(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n},(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}
.
Note that, in the LHS of the
eqs.

228
and
229
, the dimensions of the systems
\mathcal{B}^{n}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
do not match and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
contains
\mathcal{B}^{n}
. Here, we pad the operators (that reside in
\mathcal{B}^{n}
) with
0
matrices in the similar way mentioned in (
224
).
Thus, it directly follows that

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt{\left\lVert\rho^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}-\rho^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{A}^{\otimes 2}}}{\left|\mathcal{A}^{2}\right|}\right\rVert}_{1}

\displaystyle={\left\lVert\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\\
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|],t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{\prime}}{|x^{n},a_{r}\rangle\langle x^{n},a_{r}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{\prime}}{|y^{n},b_{t}\rangle\langle y^{n},b_{t}|}\otimes\left(\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),s^{n},\delta}-\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}}\right)\right\rVert}_{1}

\displaystyle\overset{a}{=}\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\\
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|],t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{\prime}}{|x^{n},a_{r}\rangle\langle x^{n},a_{r}|}\otimes P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{\prime}}{|y^{n},b_{t}\rangle\langle y^{n},b_{t}|}\otimes{\left\lVert\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),s^{n},\delta}-\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}}\right\rVert}_{1}\overset{b}{\leq}8\delta,

(230)

where
a
follows from direct sum property
[
49
, eq. (
2.2.97
)]
of Schatten-
l_{1}
norm and
b
follows from (
228
). Similarly using (
229
), we have the following relation;

\displaystyle{\left\lVert\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}}-\sigma^{X^{n}Y^{n}\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{A}^{\otimes 2}}}{\left|\mathcal{A}^{2}\right|}\right\rVert}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 8\delta.

(231)

C-D

Construction of the “Intersection Projector”

It follows from
eqs.

263
,
264
and
265
that
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X},\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
have the following form:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X,x^{n},y^{n}},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y,x^{n},y^{n}},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}\overset{X^{n}}{|x^{n}\rangle\langle x^{n}|}\otimes\overset{Y^{n}}{|y^{n}\rangle\langle y^{n}|}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,x^{n},y^{n}}.

For each
(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n}
, we denote
\mathcal{W}_{X,x^{n},y^{n}},\mathcal{W}_{Y,x^{n},y^{n}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{x^{n},y^{n}}
to be the support spaces of orthogonal complements of
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X,x^{n},y^{n}},\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y,x^{n},y^{n}}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,x^{n},y^{n}}
respectively.
For each
(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n}
, our objective is to construct a measurement that behaves like an intersection of
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,X,x^{n},y^{n}},\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,Y,x^{n},y^{n}}
and
\hat{\mathbb{T}}_{n,x^{n},y^{n}}
. The sole idea is to find the complement of the span of
\mathcal{W}_{X,x^{n},y^{n}},\mathcal{W}_{Y,x^{n},y^{n}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{x^{n},y^{n}}
.
However, there is a high possibility that the span of
\mathcal{W}_{X,x^{n},y^{n}},\mathcal{W}_{Y,x^{n},y^{n}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{x^{n},y^{n}}
can cover the whole Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
.
Thus, to resolve this issue, not only do we need to enlarge the Hilbert space but also tilt at least two of them in all possible pairs of orthogonal directions i.e. each from the collection
\{a_{r},b_{t}\}_{(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}}
. For each
(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n}
and
(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}
, we define the following tilted subspaces:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{X,(x^{n},a_{r}),y^{n},\delta}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{M}_{X,a_{r},\delta}(\mathcal{W}_{X,x^{n},y^{n}}),

(232)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{Y,x^{n},(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{M}_{Y,b_{t},\delta}(\mathcal{W}_{Y,x^{n},y^{n}}),

(233)

where
\delta\in(0,1)
is a free parameter, which can be finetuned.
Note that for each
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n},(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}
,
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{X,(x^{n},a_{r}),y^{n}\delta}
,

\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{Y,x^{n},(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}
and
\mathcal{W}_{x^{n},y^{n}}
reside inside

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{X}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{Y}}
and

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
respectively and all three of them reside inside
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
. For each
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n},(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}
, we define the following span of subspaces:

\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}:=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{X,(x^{n},a_{r}),y^{n}\delta}\bigplus\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{Y,x^{n},(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}\bigplus\mathcal{W}_{x^{n},y^{n}},

(234)

which is considered to be the span of
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{X,(x^{n},a_{r}),y^{n}\delta},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{Y,x^{n},(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}
and

\mathcal{W}_{x^{n},y^{n}}
(we denote
\bigplus
as the span of subspaces).
We consider
\Pi_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{(x^{n},a_{X_{r}}),(y^{n},a_{Y_{t}}),\delta}}
to be the orthogonal projection in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
onto the spanned subspace

\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{(x^{n},a_{X.r}),(y^{n},a_{Y.t}),\delta}
. Thus, for each possible
(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n},(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}
, we define the following POVM element as follows

\mathbb{T}^{\star}_{n,(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}:=\left(\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}-\Pi_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}}\right)\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}}\left(\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}-\Pi_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}}\right),

(235)

where
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}}

is a orthogonal projector in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
onto
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
.
We have to incorporate the collection of the POVMs
\left\{\mathbb{T}^{\star}_{n,(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}\right\}
into a single POVM in the following manner:

{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\\
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|],t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]\end{subarray}}\overset{X^{\prime}}{|x^{n},a_{r}\rangle\langle x^{n},a_{r}|}\otimes\overset{Y^{\prime}}{|y^{n},b_{t}\rangle\langle y^{n},b_{t}|}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\star}_{n,(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}.

(236)

C-E

Smoothness of the tilting maps

For an arbitrary collection
\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}_{(x^{n},y^{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n}}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
, we consider the following marginals of the state
\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}}
:

\displaystyle\sigma^{X^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Tr}_{Y^{\prime}}\left[\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}}\right]=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{\prime}}{|x^{n},a_{r}\rangle\langle x^{n},a_{r}|}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),\delta},

(237)

\displaystyle\sigma^{Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}\}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Tr}_{X^{\prime}}\left[\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}}\right]=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n},t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{\prime}}{|y^{n},b_{t}\rangle\langle y^{n},b_{t}|}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta},

(238)

\displaystyle\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Tr}_{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}}\left[\sigma^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}}\right]=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\\
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|],t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta},

(239)

where for each
x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]
and
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n},t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]
we have

\displaystyle\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),\delta}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\sum_{y^{n},t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta},

\displaystyle\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\sum_{x^{n},r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta},

and we define the following marginals of
\rho^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}
:

\displaystyle\rho^{X^{\prime}}_{P_{X}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Tr}_{Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\left[\rho^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}\right]=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},a_{X}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\overset{X^{\prime}}{|x^{n},a_{X}\rangle\langle x^{n},a_{X}|}=\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{X}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{X}}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{A}^{X}}}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|},

\displaystyle\rho^{Y^{\prime}}_{P_{Y}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Tr}_{X^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\left[\rho^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}\right]=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n},a_{Y}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\overset{Y^{\prime}}{|y^{n},a_{Y}\rangle\langle y^{n},a_{Y}|}=\rho^{\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{\otimes n}}_{P_{Y}}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{A}^{Y}}}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}.

Now, for a statevector
|h\rangle
in

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
and for any
(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}
, we apply the tilting map
\mathcal{M}_{XY,a_{X_{r}},a_{Y_{t}},\delta}
. Then, we have the following series of equalities:

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}\sum_{t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}\mathcal{M}_{XY,a_{X_{r}},a_{Y_{t}},\delta}(|h\rangle\langle h|)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|(1+2\delta^{2})}\sum_{t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}\Big((|h\rangle\oplus\delta|h\rangle|a_{r}\rangle)(\langle h|\oplus\delta\langle h|\langle a_{r}|)+\delta(|h\rangle\oplus\delta|h\rangle|a_{r}\rangle)\langle h|\langle b_{t}|\oplus\delta|h\rangle|b_{t}\rangle(\langle h|\oplus\delta\langle h|\langle a_{r}|)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt\oplus\delta^{2}|h\rangle\langle h||b_{t}\rangle\langle b_{t}|\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\delta^{2}}{1+2\delta^{2}}\mathcal{M}_{X,a_{r},\delta}(|h\rangle\langle h|)\oplus\mathcal{N}_{X,a_{r},\delta}(|h\rangle\langle h|),

(240)

where for each
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]
, we define

\mathcal{N}_{X,a_{r},\delta}(|h\rangle\langle h|):=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|(1+2\delta^{2})}\sum_{t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}\left(\delta(|h\rangle\oplus\delta|h\rangle|a_{r}\rangle)\langle h|\langle b_{t}|\oplus\delta|h\rangle|b_{t}\rangle(\langle h|\oplus\delta\langle h|\langle a_{r}|)\oplus\delta^{2}|h\rangle\langle h||b_{t}\rangle\langle b_{t}|\right).

(241)

Similarly, for each
t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{Y,b_{t},\delta}(|h\rangle\langle h|):=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|(1+2\delta^{2})}\sum_{r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}\left(\delta(|h\rangle\oplus\delta|h\rangle|b_{t}\rangle)\langle h|\langle a_{r}|\oplus\delta|h\rangle|a_{r}\rangle(\langle h|\oplus\delta\langle h|\langle b_{t}|)\oplus\delta^{2}|h\rangle\langle h||a_{r}\rangle\langle a_{r}|\right),

(242)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\delta}(|h\rangle\langle h|):=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}(1+2\delta^{2})}\sum_{r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|],t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}\big(\delta|h\rangle(\langle h|\langle a_{r}|\oplus\langle h|\langle b_{t}|)\oplus\delta(|h\rangle|a_{r}\rangle\oplus|h\rangle|b_{t}\rangle)\langle h|

\displaystyle\hskip 170.0pt\oplus\delta^{2}|h\rangle\langle h|(|a_{r}\rangle\oplus|b_{t}\rangle)(\langle a_{r}|\oplus\langle b_{t}|)\big).

(243)

For each
(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2},\delta\in(0,1)
and any
|h\rangle
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n}
, we have

\displaystyle{\left\lVert\mathcal{N}_{X,a_{r},\delta}(|h\rangle\langle h|)\right\rVert}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\delta}{\sqrt{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}},

(244)

\displaystyle{\left\lVert\mathcal{N}_{Y,b_{t},\delta}(|h\rangle\langle h|)\right\rVert}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\delta}{\sqrt{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|}},

(245)

\displaystyle{\left\lVert\mathcal{N}_{\delta}(|h\rangle\langle h|)\right\rVert}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{7\delta}{\sqrt{A}},

(246)

where we give a proof for
eqs.

244
and
246
in Appendices
I
and
J
respectively. The proof of (
245
) follows directly from the proof of (
244
). Now, from
eqs.

240
,
241
,
242
and
243
, for each
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]
and
t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]
, and for any collection
\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(x^{n},a_{r}),\delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\delta^{2}}{1+2\delta^{2}}\mathcal{M}_{X,a_{r},\delta}(\sum_{y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}}P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}})\oplus\mathcal{N}_{X,a_{r},\delta}(\sum_{y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}}P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}),

(247)

\displaystyle\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\delta^{2}}{1+2\delta^{2}}\mathcal{M}_{Y,b_{t},\delta}(\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}})\oplus\mathcal{N}_{Y,b_{t},\delta}(\sum_{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}),

(248)

\displaystyle\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+2\delta^{2}}(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}})\oplus\mathcal{N}_{\delta}(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\\
y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n}}).

(249)

Hence, from
eqs.

247
,
248
and
249
and
eqs.

244
,
245
and
246
, we conclude that a quantum state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})
can be tilted along
2
mutually orthogonal directions and has
\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}
such choice of directions each of
\{(a_{r},b_{t})\}_{(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}}
. Now consider

\{a_{r}\}_{r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}
and tilt the state
\rho
towards each elements of
\{a_{r},b_{\bar{t}}\}_{r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}
for a fixed
\bar{t}\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]
.
Then, if we take a uniform mixture of these tilted states,
the resultant state will only be primarily tilted towards the direction of
b_{\bar{t}}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}^{Y}}
. The above phenomenon holds for
\{b_{t}\}_{t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]}
and both
\{(a_{r},b_{t})\}_{(r,t)\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]^{2}}
as well.
In the following section, we prove
eqs.

107
,
104
,
105
and
106
, for the intersection projector
\mathbb{T}^{\star}_{n}
with respect to the collections of tilted states

\left\{\rho^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}\right\}_{s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}}
,
\{\rho^{Y^{\prime}}_{P_{Y}}\otimes\sigma^{X^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\}}\}_{\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n}}\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})}
,
\{\rho^{X^{\prime}}_{P_{X}}\otimes\sigma^{Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{Y},\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}\}}\}_{\{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{y^{n}}\}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})}
and
\{\rho^{X^{\prime}}_{P_{X}}\otimes\rho^{Y^{\prime}}_{P_{Y}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\}_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\otimes n})}
,
which are basically the tilted and augmented versions of the collections
H_{0},H_{1,Y},H_{1,X},H_{1}
mentioned in section
eqs.

48
,
49
,
50
and
51
.

C-F

Performance analysis of
{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}

Now, for any
s^{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
, we directly upper-bound the error probability
\mathrm{Tr}\big[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}-{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}\right)

\rho^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}\big]
as follows

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbb{I}^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}-{\mathbb{T}}^{\star}_{n}\right)\rho^{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{P_{X},P_{Y},s^{n}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\\

\displaystyle\overset{a}{\leq}\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\\
r\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|],t\in[\left|\mathcal{A}\right|]\end{subarray}}P^{n}_{X}(x^{n})P^{n}_{Y}(y^{n})\left(\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\mathbb{I}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}-\mathbb{T}^{\star}_{n,(x^{n},a_{r}),(y^{n},b_{t}),\delta})\rho^{\mathcal{B}^{n}}_{x^{n},y^{n},s^{n}}\right]\right)+4\delta

\displaystyle\overset{b}{\leq}\frac{4}{\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\\
